HINTS AND INTERESTING FACTS AND CHAT FOR AMATEURS.

allowed to lay naturally. If forced, a goodly number of infertile eggs will result. Laying and breeding stock requite two meals a day. This usually takes the form of a soft mash in the morning, and grain in the evening, although confined birds should be. allowed greens at mid-day, and a little grain to scratch for. House-scraps should be used with care. Bread and potatoes and similar starchy articles are poor egg-foods. Their value is increased considerably when they include bits of lean meat, ecc.. from the table. Mixed with such foods as bran, biscuit meal, and sharps, they tend to cheapen the food bill without inter- fering very much with the values ef such foods, providing they are utilised discreetly. A con- stant changing of diet, both in mash and grain will help to keep the hens healthy. Bran is very indigestible unless well scalded.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
HINTS FOR BEGINNERS.

Keep the young stock going all the while. -Dispose of any unprofitable surplus hens not required for next season. Hens can be placed together so that certain runs can be rested. When grouping up the hens see that they are not overcrowded.

FANCY AND UTILITY.

The Lawn Clippings.

Lawn clippings are excellent for young chicks. Those who have friends that possess lawns should ask them to hand over the fine lawn clippings. Give the latter at mid-day.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
Dig the Runs Up.

Backyarders should make a point of spading up their runs' once a week. The fowls will enjoy the fresh soil and the run will always look much neater. Turn the ground well over and bury a few grains for the fowls to scratch for.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
The Yearly Dig.

Once a year it is wise for backyarders to re- new the earth in the run. Take out the soil to the depth of six inches or more and replace with fresh soil. Avoid Draughty Houses.

Just now ailments will be common if the houses are draughty. The structures must be well ventilated, but there is a lot of difference between " fresh " air and "draughts." The former is necessary.—" Poultry World."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
FOREIGN EGGS.

I have seen hundreds of cases of foreign eggs that contain beautifully large brown eggs of an even sample throughout the whole case, says a writer in "The Feathered World," none of which had, in my opinion, from the samples I broke, been laid more than three or four days; and were far fresher than the majority of Eng- lish eggs tested at the same time. I would undertake to say that you would hardly find a single English consumer who, when eggs were worth retail, say, 2d. apiece, would give that for a foreign egg if they could buy an English one for nd. or even 3d. Rightly or wrongly—wrongly in very many eases—the foreigu egg has the very worst repute that is possible for an edible article to obtain, and if that- one word " foreign " were compulsorily stamped on every egg imported into this coun- try the price of new-laid English eggs would go 'up by leaps and'bounds. Failing the ability to get this done, poultry-keepers ought at least to agitate for the name of the country from which it is imported being imprinted on each egg, and a heavy penalty imposed on anyone who removes the same or obliterates it. Surely poultry breeders could organise a huge petition to be presented at the House of Com- mons, and individuals could induce their Own Members 'of, Parliament to press for a Bill on the subject to be passed?

===== 0002601_19150605_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ANNUAL MOULT.

The annual period of the casting of the feathers is attended by dangers to the health of poultry which are, as a rule, neither suffi- ciently realised nor guarded against, for the rapid shedding and comparatively slow re- newal of the feathers renders the fowls pecu- liarly liable to catarrhal arid lung diseases. Dry, warm roosting quarters and shelter from cold winds will reduce these risks. But that is not enough. The successful manufacture of a new outfit of feathers, composed, as they al- most entirely are, of nitrogen and mineral salts, means that the blood must be richly supplied with these elements.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
The Diet.

In the early stages of the moulting period the foods supplied require to be rather low both in quality and in quantity, because the aim is to encourage the falling of the feathers. A useful ration for, morning feeding consists of three-parts middlings, one pea or bean meal, and one bran; in the afternoon a small quantity of wheat, oats, or dari should be provided. Foods 'to be avoided during the moult are barley, maize, rice and hempseed. Immediately the new feathers are being formed, however, the foods should be changed, and-those of a concentrated character supplied. Meals and grains rich in albuminous or nitro- genous matter should be employed, as these assist so greatly in the formation of feathers; carbo-hydrates and fat are not required at this period, and foods containing a generous proportion of these elements should be avoided. A good ration for use first thing in the morn- ing consists of four-parts middlings, two ground oats, and one pea or bean meal. Pea and bean meals are extremely rich in nitro- genous matter, and they serve a useful pur- pose at such a time. An occasional feed of split beans is very beneficial, and greatly en- tourages the new growth of feathers. liberal supply of green food is extremely im- portant. Lettuces, boiled stinging nettles, or the tender leaves of cabbages are all useful.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
A Tonic Necessary.

While hens are moulting I have always found it a good plan to give them a little sulphate of iron and sulphuric acid, as both have a beneficial effect, and hasten the process somewhat. The simplest way to give it is by adding to the drinking water in the proportion of one piece of sulphate of iron the size of a walnut and ten drops of sulphuric acid to a gallon of water. A tablespoonful of powdered flowers of sulphur for every twenty birds keeps them in good condition. A dust bci.th of wood ashes in which a handful of powdered sulphur has been mixed will help to banish parasites,' and also provide the mineral salts for the new feathers.—" Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
HENS IN CONFINEMENT.

liens in confinement must be supplied with such necessaries as their more fortunate sisters with unlimited runs can find for them- selves from spring - to autumn, says the "Poultry World." I refer to greens, insects and grubs. The former are easy enough to supply; cabbage, lettuce and swedes (cut in two), &c., will be appreciated. If suspended in the run they will induce exercise, but it is not wise to throw them on the ground, as they may be fouled. Greens should be given to all classes of poultry during the winter. Meat takes the place of insects, and the supply should be regulated according to the amount of insect life , available. Breeding stock shotdd be

!V!!e.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
POULTRY IN THE GARDEN.

Poultry-keeping and gardening can be and should be profitably combined, and it will well repay gardeners and fruit growers to look into the possibilities of this combination. The valne of poultry manure alone makes the pro- posal attractive, apart from the profit to be earned direct from the fowls: Various schemes have been formulated for accommodating poultry in gardens, but the most economical is that which embraces the principle of alternate stocking and cropping. Plans have been published showing how a square or nearly square plot of ground" can be divided into four enclosures of equal extent by means of wire netting fences, with a house in the middle, so that each plot may be stocked in turn while the other three are bear- ing crops.—" Agricultural Economist and Hor- ticultural Review."

THE POULTRY PEN.

VEGETARIANISM (.01IING •.)

Rel. A. M. Mitchell Warns Guardians To Get Ready.

At Wednesday's meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians, the Rev. A. M. Mitchell alluded to the rising price of meat, and sug- gested that vegetarianism was coming. The Rev. A M. Mitchell said it seemed in- evitable that the Board would shortly have to introduce a good deal of vegetarian fare into the diet in the House and the Cottage Homes.. The cooks ought to be given the tip to get themselves ready to prepare vegetarian dishes. Unless the cooks rose to the occasion the in- mates might get into a state of rebellion when vegetarian diet became necessary. He 'did not know whether the Guardians would like to adopt vegetarianism for themselves. Per- sonally, he had tried it with success for 20 years.

A Guardian ventured a jocular query of the word "success," but the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, unabashed by the good-humoured personality, went on to say ' that the world had laughed at him for twenty years, and now his turn .was coming to laugh at the world. (Laughter.) It might be a good plan, said the vegetarian Vicar, to try the no-meat diet at the Cottage Homes at once. The Chairman (Mr. A. S. Jolley) said he did not think. the time was ripe.

NEW FORM OF MEASLES.

A new and highly virulent form of measles Nvas described on , Tuesday at Lambeth Cor- oner's Court, when an inquest was held on a child of eight. Dr. B. Salisbury Trevor, pathologist, said that an ocular examination could easily have mistaken the case for .one of " spotted " fever. This form of measles, of which there had been three or four cases lately, was technically known as " fulminating,". and was of a very violent.character.

WA.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 5, 1915.

STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES

TRAM-DRIVER AT THE

FRONT.

"'tie Want Them All."

Councillor Hens-hall, the popular representa- tive of Latchford Ward, has received two highly interesting letters from Sergt. E. J. Widocks, with the machine gun section of. the Warring- ton Territorials, in the trenches "somewhere in Belgium," who, when at home, lives with his wife in Knutsford-road, and is engaged as a motorman on the Latchford, tram route. He says, under date May 18 : "I received your kind letter safely whilst in the trenches, and on going out last Saturday night I found your very nice present awaiting me. We came back in again on Monday night, so I had to bring the box, &c., with me, and I and the machine gun section are going to par- take of your hospitality at tea-time to-night— that is, if the Huns in front don't 'gas' us, or burst the box with a shell. I have got it well in a corner of my 'dug-out' (you see I have gone back to prehistoric days and live like the ancient Britons) and I hardly think their whizz bangs' will find it. Owing to a new ar- rangement we only had two days out this time, so we are now in for four days and nights and then out again for the same period. I came in last time on May Ist, so you see we were 15 days in—the longest time yet.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
Shave Under Shell Fire.

"We are in a trench that gets shelled regu- larly. I was in the middle of a much-needed shave on Saturday last and had just got the I razor going, when over they came, and I had to finish the operation in between the shells. You will see the old Battalion has suffered• pretty much, and we have had nearly 40 more casual- ties (the great part wounds only). Honestly speaking, the Warrington public haye no cause to be ashamed of the old 4th, and I don't think the brave lads at Market Gate would call us 'Saturday night soldiers' now. We want them all out here to see this thing through. You will see that the enemy stops at nothing to gain his end. We shall beat them in the end, but every man should join who possibly can. We must win over such an unscrupulous enemy. You know the saying, 'The mills of God grind slowly," &c. You have no idea at all of the desolation and ruin, and I could not convey the picture to you in a letter. It is really and truly shocking. I can't tell you where we are, or much else about the war, as we are not at liberty to do so, but some day in the future I hope to tell you a few yarns about our adven- tures.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
Glad the Bridge is Finished.

"I am glad the Bridge is finished. It is a fine sight, as I have seen a photo of it in the trenches ! I daresay things in Warrington are somewhat quiet. I shall be glad to get back again—even if some rather eccentric carter gets on the track and causes my temper to rise. We 'don't complain, but it is a real hard life here. It is nerve racking, to put it mildly." "I wonder what America will do over the Lusitania trouble? Talk, I presume. I dare- say I have been to some places that you visited when here on your holiday. "I received the ' Annual ' from Mr. Alderman Bennett, and would you kindly thank him for me? I will write to him the first opportunity. Also remember me to Councillor Archer and Councillor Wilson, and all others. "It is now raining heavily, and the trench is horribly wet and muddy. This is being written on my knee, and my back is aching. I think the wet has depressed our ' friends ' opposite, as they are rather quiet, and are not sending us many souvenirs across.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
" I Hope to be in Warrington -"

"The maxim is all ready. for action, so 'let 'em all come.' This must be all this time. ' Rather in uninteresting epistle I know, but the subject is one .over which we have to be careful. I don't know when it will be over, but I hope to be in Warrington by next Noyem- ber twelvemonth! I think that is right. Jok- ing asicle, though, I think it won't last a great longer. I- hope both you and Mrs. Hen- shall are well. I am in the 'very best of health myself. Once again, with the best of wishes and the kindest regards to all."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
WHERE HOUSES ARE CHEAP.

• In an earlier letter Sergt. Widocks .wrote to Councillor Henshall as one of his "burgesses," saying the Battalion were all well and in good spirits, "awaiting the chance to make our name." At that time the mud was "worse than the Germans," but the men were supplied with top boots to keep them dry. "I see the rates are up," he goes on; "I sup- pose they will raise the rents. If so, there is a whole village, and a big one, close to us that the Warrington people might have at a very low rent if they like to chance the shells, which are daily visitors. Most of the houses are roof- less, and would want renovating. I don't quite know who holds the keys for them.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
Does not Favour Lady Drivers.

"I suppose you have fresh faces ,on the cars by this, as the friends of mine on them tell me a lot have joined the colours. . . . At one place I passed through I saw a lady driver of an electric ear. Please don't introduce this system in Warrington. "I have lost 191 b. in weight, so this war has done some good."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
A SLIGHT " NIFF " OF GAS.

Warrington " Terrier " Tells How It

Makes The Eyes Smart,

Private L. Houghton. of No. 1 Platoon of the 4th South Lancashire (Warrington Territorials), whose home is at 36, Pickmere-street, 'Warring- ton, writing to a friend in town, under date May 29th, says: "We are having a rest out of the trenches, and the spot where we are located is a beautiful one. The weather is grand, and everything is looking well. As I write the big guns are speaking, but they speak a lot louder when an attack is on. Whilst we were in the trenches last time we had a slight niff ' of the gas and I can assure you that it was not at all grand. It makes the eyes smart awfully, and you cannot help but rub them.

" Terriers' " Busy Nights.

" Every night we go to the trenches, either to dig, or to take rations, water, sandbags, coke, duck-boards for walling up, &c. As you will gather, therefore, our tithe' iwpretty fully occu- pied at night, but when not actually doing a snell in the trenches, we rest during the day. The food is 'not at all bad, Init we must not drink- water unless it has first been boiled."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
SUNDAY SERVICES AT THE

FRONT.

Work Of Soldier-Member Of Working

Men's Mission.

In a letter to his class-mates associated with the Young Men's Class at the Working Men's Mission, -Private Leonard Roberts, of 20, Thew- -Hs-street, thanked them for the note of cheer which he received while he was on duty in the trenches: He was glad that he had responded to the call of King and country; and was de- lighted to 'hear that so many of his fellow- members had also joined the colours. The "boys" were passing through many trying experiences at the Front, but despite them all they were presenting a bold front to the enemy and trusting in the Divine Providence to come through successfully. He had .met many War- rington companions from time to time, and a number of them had managed to remain to- gether. They were, as far as possible, conduct- ing a series, of Sunday services, which were proving a means of inspiration and blessing. In concluding his letter, the gallant private stated that he was looking forward to being able, at no distant date, to be back again in Warrington to enjoy the pleasures of the Sunday afternoon class.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
GERNLIN GAS.

" Not Pleasant Stuff To Take," Says Rifleman Riley.

Miss Riley, of 6, Howard-street, Warrington, has received a letter from her brother, Rifleman John Riley, of A Company, 3rd King's Royal Rifles, now serving at the Front, in which he asks her td, "do an unpleasant job," adding: "Dick Harman asked me some time ago if I would write and let his girl know if anything happened to him. Well, I am sorry to say he has been killed and I am asking you to let the poor girl know the news. He was hit in the head on Sunday, May 9th, and killed instantly. He was a good lad and feared nothing, and was a great favourite among his comrades. Don't get anxious about me, because I only go up to the firing line at night, when I take the food up for the lads."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
Been Having It a Bit Rough.

In a later letter Rifleman Riley says: "I sup- pose you can see from the papers that our lads have been having it a bit rough out here this last month, but we do not mind, for you can bet we are on the winning side. I think they have got tired of using their gas, for since their reverse at Of course, we do not want it, for it is not pleasant stuff to take. "We have got lots of souvenirs from the Ger- mans, but the trouble is to get them home."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
"CAUGHT 'EM ON TIIE 110 P."

How Trench-full Of " Tommie,," • Trapped The Huns.

Driver W. J. IL Beckett, of the 87th Battery of the Royal Field Artillery, writing to friends in Warrington—Mr. and Mrs. Lloyd, of 36, Wakefield-street—acknowledges the receipt of an "Examiner" and says: "A lot of familiar faces appear among the pictures this week. I see that one chap whom I knew very well has been killed whilst doing his duty with the South Lancashires. What a terrible blow it must have been when Mrs. Eyres, of Vere- street, first heard the tidings. 'I sympathise deeply with her and the poor children. Private Eyres was one of the many friends. I made when I frequented the Junior Consvrvative Club in Orford-lane."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
"A Mean, Cowardly Action."

The writer goes on to refer to the sinking of the Lusitania and observes: "I did not dream that even the Germhuns could be guilty of such a mean, cowardly action. But they'll suffer for it; indeed, we are busy putting them through it at the present moment. We have done nothing but shell them ever since we heard the news. Their gas-dodge is just about played out now. Our boys are well guarded against the fumes—you May bet your life on that! Three Germany's rolled into one would not be able to poison the little British Army. We caught some of them on the hop the other day, when they were using the gas-bombs. Our boys simply lay down in the trenches with 'their respirators on and the Germhuns poured their poisoning gases into their midst. A Surprise Packet for the Germans.

"Seeing no sign of activity, the Germhuns concluded that they had poisoned all our boys. So they ceased fire and simply sauntered over to our trenches, expecting to find them full of dead and to jump into them comfortably and easily. But they were badly deceived, for as soon as they reached the entanglements our lads suddenly opened a murderous fire on them and literally mowed them down. That was good biz ' ! Eh, what? . . . We are simply giving the Huns socks! correct that Italy has joined in? If so, it should shorten this terrible business."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
wan THE A.S.C. IN EGYPT.

Corporal FranlhTav Describes Life 0 An Eastern Outpost Depot.

Corporal Frank Kay, of the Army Service Corps, who will be known to many people of all classes in Warrington as a member of our reporting staff, who volunteered at the com- mencement of the present year, and who is now serving with the Mediterranean Expeditionary Force in Egypt, recently penned a long and in- teresting letter to his wife, who resides at 209, Wilderspool-causeway, in the course oi which he says:—

"Our new camp is near.the sea, but also near the filthy native compounds. . . . The heat has been awful. The first night here . . . I was corporal of the guard, which meant that I had to keep awake . . . and see that the guards were all right for twenty-four hours. Nice job, eh? For the next few days it was work practically night and day, getting foi4lige and provisions in.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
A Terrible Sandstorm.

"On May 13th there was a terrible sandstorm, and we were working in a wooden shed, when the roof was blown clean off by the wind. I shall never forget our trying to force our way back to the tents, through the blinding, scorch- ing sand. The tents were nearly blown away, and I can tell you we were thankful when com- paratively peaceful morning succeeded the roughness of the night. There were refugees froin Palestine all round us, but most of the men folk joined up and formed a camel corps. They were soon sent away, and it was awful to see the women crying and struggling to get at their husbands and sweethearts, as they marched past with the camels. They. had just been driven out of Palestine by the Turks, and now they were being separated from those they loved. , They have no homes at all, poor creatures, but just live out in the open around here as well as they can. Hell cannot be much to fear, when one has in mind the experience of a war like this, a war which has caused so much suffering, pain and anxiety. After we had fin- ished issuing war materials at -, five of us were sent to , a- few miles away, to start a depot there, and it is from that place that I write. On April 20th I was sent up with a dozen Arabs to get the compound for our depot planned out, and I was kept 'busy at it ' all

day. . • •

===== 0002601_19150605_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
" WORKING DAY AND NIGHT."

" Tommy " Tells Of Mouth-Pads As Protection Against 6as-Bombs.

Writing to a friend, Mr. Leach, at Grappen- hall, Private Wallace McHugh, of C Company, sth Battalion of the Cheshire Regiment, under date May 19th, says : - -

"We have had it rather hard of late, working night and day. I expect you will have heard of the fierce fighting there has been in these parts. . . . We have been supplied with pads to put over our mouths, as the enemy are using their poisonous gases here. . . . Last time we were in the trenches we had several casualties; in- deed, we had the misfortune to lose our platoon commander, Lieutenant Walker. He was a good officer to his men, and he came from Southport and joined us at Northampton."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
SAD SIGHT FOR SOLDIERS.

Dead Both 01 Woman In Barn.

In a recent letter to Mr. Rupert Jones, of 44, Baxter-street, Private J. Morgan, who is serving with "A" Company of the Warring- ton Territorials, says:— " Since coming out here we have all under- gone some very trying experiences, besides witnessing some very pitiful sights The other day the village in which we were staying was shelled, and the inhabitants, who, I should think, numbered about a thousand, rushed out into the streets in a state of great terror. Some of the younger were helping the aged, and others carried the children. Several of the poor folk were hit, a few of them fatally. We had occasion to visit an old barn 'way back behind the firing line, and there we discovered a 'woman's body, with a child lying across it. It was one of the saddest sights I have seen

How " Tommy " Keeps Up His "Pecker."

"We often have a sing-song between our- selves; we need something to take our minds off such ghastly sights. . . . My cousin, Private Minshull, had the misfortune to be hit the other day, but I am glad to say that his hurt is not serious I often_ come

_ across J. Brimelow and E. Thomas, and they both look 'in the pink." Private Morgan's home is at 33, Liverpool- road, and he was at one time a member of the Young Men's Bible Class at the Working Men's Mission.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SINGING SOLDIERS.

Kitchener's Men March Into Fighting- Line Chanting Panto. Songs.

Driver J. Beckett, of the 87th Battery of the Royal Field Artillery, writing from "some- where in France" to friends in Warrington, men- tions the delight with which he receives a copy of "The Examiner" each week and the amuse- ment which the publication therein of one of his letters descriptive of his battery's attempts at "fag making" caused, and adds: "Such chaps as those who comprise Kitchener's Army ought to make a big hole in 'the German fight- ing machine.' . . •

Kitchener's Jolly Boys.

"They are a folly set of fellows, and do not fear danger at all. It was a great joy to us to see them marching into our lines and hear them singing new songs , and ditties from the old country. Those chaps must have been lucky enough to witness last winter's pantomimes, and they have certainly 'caught on' to all the catchy songs. I want to see them catch on just as readily to the mean, cowardly Germhuns.' . . . What a terrible deed the sinking of the Lusitania was. To destroy so many innocent, sweet, precious lives! Such deeds are the deeds of vermin! . . . But the Gerrahuns ' have to reckon with us yet for that business . . .

and we are making it jolly well hot for them now. . . .

When Our Guns Speak -!

"When our guns speak it is enough to waken the dead . . . and when they speak together all along the line—every gun speaking for itself with no uncertain voice—the Germhun ' fight- ing machine will shiver and break into small particles, which will .be swept up by our gal- lant infantry and cavalry. . . . I hope my appeal for fags (made in a previous letter) will be answered by some of my friends in Warring- ton. By the way, I am the only representative of Warrington in this battery."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
AFTER TIIE LUSITANIA MURDERS.

Warringtonian Sees Sight Ile Will

Never Forget.

Writing ,to Mr. Rupert Jones, of 44, Baxter- street, Warrington, Gunner H. Thomson, of a Depot Company of the Royal Garrison Artil- lery, whose home is in Wellfield-street, and who is at present stationed in the South of Ireland, asks the recipient of his letter to convey to the members of the Bible-class in connection with the Working Men's Mission, Bank Quay, his thanks for the kindly message forwarded him by his fellow members of the class.

"It is a comfort," he writes, "to know that there are mates at home thinking of you and praying for your welfare. It gives one fresh courage to go forward and fight life's battle, as well as the battle for King and country." He goes on to refer to his last visit to the Mission, and hopes that it will not be long before he is again able to attend service therein, although he is afraid that the time will not be so 'short as he could wish. "This war," he says, "is going to be a long one, and every fit man will be needed if we are, ultimately, to be in a posi- tion to claim the victory. . . . I believe we are in the right, and I am leaving myself in God's hands, knowing that He will look after me and protect me."

"England Must Wake Up More and More."

"Terrible, indeed, was the sight in

after the sinking of the Lusitania. In the lug- gage office of the Cunard Company I saw stretched out three long rows of the bodies of innocent men and women and children, of all nationalities; all murdered by the barbarous Huns. God grant that I May never be called updn to witness such a sight again. But, with all their outrages, I do not believe that the Germans can possibly win, though England must wake up more and.-more, and get a busi- ness Government, or woe betide her !"

===== 0002601_19150605_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
BULLET SENT HOME.

I:)lands's Man Who Was Saved By

His Pack.

Private Henry Leather, of the Warrington Territorials, writing to his sister, Mrs. J. bolt, 39, Birchall-street, with whom he lived, tells how he was preserved from a German bullet (which he sends her) by the thickness of his pack. Private Leather, who is 25 years of age and single, was employed in the netting department of Rylands' Bros. and joined the Territorials on the outbreak of the war. He says:--

_ "The Germans began to send gas over while we were in the trenches. We have all been supplied witli masks and pads, and we ,do look a \rum lot with them on. None of us have been affected by the gas yet, as we only got some blown over from other directions. We ket our letters brought to the trenches, but we have to wait till w-e come out before we get our parcels. I would give something to be in that big bath in Warrington. "I fouricl the enclosed bullet in my pack. It was a good job it was well filled. The bullet got stuck in my oil sheet, which I had folded about eight limes, which stopped tilt,' force of it. It looks very much like one of our bullets. If it is one they must be using our ammunition. I expect they use any- thing they can get frreii hands on."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
Lonely and Hot.

"There are only the five of us here, with an officer, and we have two marquees for groceries and provisions, &c., .and two tents—one for the officer and one for us. All the forage is laid out round the sides of the compottnd. This is a very lonely place round the shore hills, and the heat is awful. The place is abso- lutely swarming with ants and other insects. . . . We are about two miles from the train terminus, and to get to that one just straddles a donkey, for the hire of which one is charged two piastres—fivepence 'in the coin of the [English] realm.' There is a big salt lake just in front of us, and when one gazes over it at night, it looks exactly like a sheet of frozen water, whilst the great stretch of sand on all sides reminds one of snow, seen under the 'tweet' lights of the near Eastern sky. Strange, indeed, to us is the fact that such' torridly hot days should be succeeded by such bitterly cold nights. . . .

===== 0002601_19150605_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
Some of the Discomforts.

"I am in charge of the forage issuing, and am at it from early in the morning Until late at night. . . . I have been stung twice by the pestiferous insects which swarm in this Part of the world, but took both bites in time, and they are ,going on well now. • . . When one of these small, but troublesome, creatures gets a hold one merely feels a sharp prick. Then the limb on which one has been bitten com- mences to well up alarmingly, and one goes off to the doctor straightaway. • . • The fight- ing in the Dardanelles is terrible. I saw a fine batch going away ready one day. and, less than seven days later, hundreds of them were back again in hospital, having been wounded in land- ing. I expect we shall' be amongst it properly before long!"

===== 0002601_19150605_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
NEWS OF THE GOOD OLD TOWN

Support The Ambulance.

Private C. C. Taylor, Warrington Terri- torials, writes to us:— " I received your valuable paper this after- noon, Monday, May 31st. It is a treat to get hold of the latest news about the dear old town. I see you have the photo of poor Frank Stokes, and also his brother, Joe. It was hard lines. I can tell you some of the sights I have seen have nearly turned my hair grey. In your paper you said the rifle-grenade was thrown. They are not thrown; they are fired lout of a rifle. It is a round tube, about 14-in. in diameter. The explosive part of the grenade is from about six to nine inches. Running from the grenade is a thin steel rod, which fits in the barrel of the rifle. It does not consist of any bullets at all. The damage is done by the explosion.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
Lost A Good Sergeant.

"We have lost another good sergeant by the name of Sergeant Griffiths. He went down the trench at midnight for water, when he got hit by a bullet in the leg. do not think it is anything serious; I hope it is not. He keeps a, baby linen shop down the bottom of Orford-lane. By the time you receive this letter I expect we will have moved further north, where all the heavy fighting is going on. We are at present having a rest. I see in your paper that the Mayoress of Warrington is making an appeal to the townspeople for a motor ambulance. I wish her every success. If the people of Warrington could only see what good work these things do they would not take a moment's thought; they would buy two or three."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
" TERRIER " MEETS ENGINEER BROTHER.

A Joyful Meeting.

Lance-Corporal Arthur Roberts, of the War- rington Territorials, son of Mr. W. J. Roberts, 42, Walker-street, and who, like his father, is in the employ of the Whitecross Company, has written to his brother, Mr. J. Roberts, Central Hotel, saying that soon after he received a letter from home saying that his brother, George, had "come. over" in the Engineers, he met some artillerymen from Aldershot, and, in answer to his inquiries, he learnt that the Engineers in which George is serving were in their division. It was joyous news. He had a stroll round and had the luck to drop across George's section—" but the greatest joy of all came when someone ran out of the ranks and, before I could turn round, our George was there. You can guess what followed. It was a meeting that I shall never forget, and I don't think George will ever forget it, either. It was hard to part, but we had the consola- tion of knowing that we had met each other on active service. He looks in the pink and the, same applies to me. Enjoyed parcel fine:"

WAR WORK ON FARMS FOR WOMEN.

At an influential meeting held at the Crewe Arms Hotel, Crewe, on Thursday, it was decided that a scheme for employment of women in agricultural and horticultural work is d?sirable. A County Committee to . work in conjunction with the Board of Trade Labour Exchanges was formed, and Lord Newton was unanimously elected chairman of it. The committee will meet at the earliest possible date to consider the scheme furthers and to organise the working of it. The Hon. John E. Cross, of High Legh, Knutsford, was appointed hon. secretary to the committee, and any information can be obtained from him or through Miss Knowles, Labour Exchanges' Divisional Office, Warrington.

Exemptions from School Attendance.

FENNINGS'

FARM AND GARDEN.

SOW MORE TURNIPS.

In view of the far greater importance attach- ing to those crops which are looked upon more directly as food for man, the swede and turnip crops will this year probably be greatly re- stricted in area, so much so that the supplY of meat may be brought into much smaller compass than it is at present. Such an event would entail almost famine prices for beef and mutton, and reveals a prospect which -must be avoided. Sow more turnips.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN AS WORKERS ON THE LAND.

As to whether their labour is as valuable ae that of the men, it is not quite fair at present to compare them. Rate per rate, I do not think they are at the present moment as good as the men, but with practice I could imagine some of them becoming of more value than ,many of the male workers. There are no doubt many things women could do as well as men in time. I do•not see why those who are fond of animals should not become able to drive horses for much farm work. Lads of 14 and 15 do much of this sort of work for us when we can get them, but we have a greater difficulty usually in securing these than in securing men.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

THISTLES. In this year of labour shortage there is 3 fear that thistles will get more grace than they would if Europe was at peace. Among the thistles which infest our lands there are several types. The Spear, Wilted, and Marsh thistles, which are all biennials, may he eat this month or next, after the plants have sent up their stems, but before the flowers open. This deals effectually with plants that are in . their second year of growth, but the seedlings should be looked for in the autumn. To spud them below the surface and turn the plants upside down is the best way of putting an end to them. The creeping thistle is the most common and troublesome of all, and its Pre' sence on arable or pasture land is an eyesore to a good farmer. This weed is a perennial, it increases itself by seeds, and also by means 1 of its creeping underground parts. There is no set time for dealing with the creeping thistle, and frequent cutting throughout the summer season, whenever growth appears, is the surest mode of eradication.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LABOURER AND THE TIED COTTAGE

A country correspondent is of opinion that the war has already decided this vexed question in favour of the tied cottage. It has, he WY's, been the sheet-anchor of agriculture, and alone saved it from drifting on to the rocks of abso- lute breakdown for want of working hands. The men in free cottages have gone in search of better pay outside the village, have left the cot- tages in the hands of women dependents and ' non-workers or even empty. Parish after Par" ish might be cited where benevolent landowners have built cottages on a non-economic basia and let thin at less than a rack-rent, only te have them empty on their hands. But the tied cottage has proved a strong link. Men earning 20s. a week and having a nice home near their work, with a bit of garden for vegetables and front for flowers, have indeed expected to share in the general prosperity of the workers; but A rise of about 10. per cent. has satisfied them, old they remain to make farming practicable. Men not tied to any place have often refused 25 ancl even 50 per cent. more money.—" Standard."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

LETTUC ES. These are. always appreciated, but it is gelifa, ally during the summer months that they most acceptable, and when all should strive te have them, in perfection. It is true that owners of suburban gardens can buy them very cheaPlY in the market, but it is equally true that when brought home market lettuces have lost Intel' of their crispness and freshness. EverybodY, therefore,, should try to grow his own lettuces, and they are by no means difficult to manage' With these, as with other crops that soon Nl' to seed, it is all important to sow often and in small quantities, thereby avoiding a glut fol" lowed by a break in the supply, which is certaill to happen if sowings are made at wide inter- vals. No crop repays better for liberal treat 7 meat. Very often the seed is sown thickly on '5 poor piece of ground and the plants dibbled 011 to soil that has had but little in the way' el manure. Those who grow lettuces thus haven° idea of the value of really well-grown produce'

Ivy. It is surprising that ivy has been so 1001 neglected and its value for covering bare walls so little recognised. At the bottom of my garden there is an unsightly shed belonging to a neje!' boar. Some few years ago at the bottom of thl° I planted some healthy young pieces of itit' which have now quite covered it. This I cti,, in regularly in April, and at the present ti'r the shed is quite hidden by a mantle of loco); fresh leaves. The old but poptilar idea tha when ivy is trained on the front of a house the walls are damp is false. It is quite true that when growths are allowed to invade the guttero they block them, causing the water to overfloW, and thus making the walls damp, but this• i 5 easily prevented. It will be found that the leaves throw off the rain and the roots draw gd moisture from the wall. How often do we fille in the case of old ivy covered walls that baci been pilled down that they are dust dry, proves the protective power of the ivy agais'so damp. The only damage likely to result Wllei ivy is trained on walls is that if the annuril trimming is neglected a rough wind may st,rl the ivy off and cause an eyesore. A plant tas.eto some time to Cover the bare place.—" The Far and Home."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER,• SATURDAY. JUNE 5, 1915.

MUNICIPAL SUPPORT FOR TRAINING CORPS.

Warrington Town Council Agree To The Principle.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
THE POTENTIALITIES OF THE ORGANISATION.

I.—That this Council cordially approves of the objects of the Warrington and District Volunteer Training Corps. 2.—That it supports the Ealing Council in urging Parliament to empower municipali- ties and District Councils to contribute towards the cost of the maintenance and equipment of any recognised organisation for purposes of home defence.

The foregoing resolutions were submitted by Alderman BENNETT at Tuesday evening's meet- ing of the Warrington Town Council, and gave rise to considerable discussion, although when the vote came to be taken there was only one dissentient—Councillor Gough.

they were called upon for service. A soldier could not be made in very much under six months, but if a man had only had one month's training he was a better man than one who had had no training at all. He had a certain amount of familiarity with drill and an under- standing of discipline, and he could carry out, to the extent of his knowledge and training, better than a man who had neither the one nor the other, the duties which might be allotted to him. It appeared that they were not even yet fully alive to the extreme gravity of the posi- tion. The entry into the war during the last few months of so many nations who, at any rate, were not positively anticipated as coming in, and the possible entry of still more, and, in consequence, the probable prolongation of the awful period of warfare, would make demands upon the manhood and material interests of the country to an extent of which nobody at the present moment had the slightest concep- tion.

Chances of Invasion Remote—But Still Chances: Any movement, which was originated for the purpose of home defence, and which, to the extent of the numbers joining it, might liberate men for service abroad, ought to have their cor- dial and unanimous support The issues at stake were so vital. He thought the chances of in- vasion were remote, but there were still chances, and, whilst thinking of them, he was of opinion that they had to thank the man who recently gave up the seals of office as head of the Admiralty that a precipitate and arranged invasion did not take place within the first month of the war. By a magnificent act of strategy on the part of those responsible for ,the posting of the Fleet in its proper position that was made impossible at that time, and he did not think anything was likely to occur, oilier than perhaps a raid on a gigantic scale. But in view of the awful frightfulness with which the war was being conducted by their enemies, could they imagine, if they had an opportunity for a short time, in a comparatively small space, • of wreaking upon English men, women and children the intense hatred and bitterness which they felt towards the people of this land, what a terrible condition of' things would obtain? They 'ought to hold out their hands and give every encouragement and assistance in their power to *those who, unable for many reasons to serve abroad, were willing to take the chance of risking life and limb in defence of the country.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT THE CORPS IS FORMED FOR.

In urging the resolutions upon the Council, the Alderman said that if the objects of the Training Corps were not pretty well understood, all he could say was that they ought to be. The corps had been in existence almost since the commencement of the war, and its functions were, like those of similar corps up and down the country, to supplement the Regular Forces of the Crown in case of need, by making proper preparations for home defence. It was an en- tirely voluntary force, and its work involved a considerable, amount of sacrifice, which was being cheerfully borne by those who were associated with the movement. The organisa- tion of the corps, which was affiliated with the Central Association of Volunteer Training Corps, was such that it could not in any way prevent or interfere with recruiting. Some of the original members had actually joined the Army, and he took it that no one under 40 years of age would now be eligible for membership unless he was prepared to sign an undertaking to enlist in the Regular Forces if called upon to do so.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
Vital arid Increasing Need for Such Organisations.

It might be urged that there was no need for a corps of this description, but he considered that there was a vital and increasing need for such organisations, for the war was assuming more gigantic and serious proportions than any of them dreamed of at the outset. They could not even see the end of it; they did not know what terrible possibilities might be in front of them. They knew that their own little Army was holding only 30 miles Out of something like 300 miles on the frontiers of France and Bel- gium, and the cry was continually for more men and more munitions. If there was a voluntary organisation in England capable of turning something like 1,000,000 men into the field for home defence, it would release many men of the Regular Army to help their brothers in the trenches. The cry for conscription became louder every day, and although, personally, he believed in it, he would be glad to see the war carried through by voluntary effort, if possible, and one way to make that possible was to have behind the Army a, huge reserve force of volun- teers. At the present moment the training corps affiliated with the Central Association boasted that they had a membership of some- thing like 1,000,000, and suet)." a force might be of enormous advantage in case of invasion, which was, perhaps, not such a remote contingency as they had been led to believe, as witness the- Zeppelin raid of the other day.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
In Case of Invasion.

That might be the forerunner of more serious raids, and, in such a pass, it might be the salva- tion of the country to have a large body of trained volunteers, ready and capable of deal- ing with any of the enemy who -might get a foothold. Some people said these training corps were not going •to be of any use. He differed. Men did not give up two or three nights a week to drill and preparation if they only meant fun. No; in this matter the stage of ridicule had been passed, and the stage at which support, enthusiasm and encouragement were needed, had been reached. The movement, he con- tended, had not met in Warrington with the support to which it was entitled. There were people who derided it and treated it as a huge joke, .whilst the response to the recent appeal for subscriptions had been meagre in the ex- treme; he felt inclined to say niggardly to a degree; for the only response was a miserable oi.2s—a downright disgrace to the town! As to the membership, a minimum of a thousand would be very moderate for a town of the size of Warrington, and he thought it was a further downright disgrace to the place that they could not muster anything like that number. He began to wonder whether the people of War- rington were really desirous of the success of their country in this war, and one way in which they could show that they .were was by help- ing this corps by personal encouragement and 'another by subscribing towards it. Lethargic Warringtonians.

He doubted sometimes whether the trumpet of the Angel Gabriel would be able to wake Warrington up. If, however, the echo of the thunderbolts that were falling on the Continent did not arouse even the most stoical and unre- sponsive person his case must be hopeless in- deed. "Are we still to go on with our games and pleasures and pleasant equable everyday life and think of nothing else?" asked Alderman Bennett. "Is it nothing to you, all ye that pass by, that all these things are happening? I refuse to think so meanly of my fellow citizens." Councillor ROBERTS seconded.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
VALUE OF THE CORPS A MATTER OF OPINION. •

• The Dr.ruiY-MAYoR Said that- the real value of the corps was a matter of opinion. When the replies were received to questions which were being put to the members as to specific duties they were prepared to undertake, they would have a better idea as to how far the corps might be utilised for the defence of the country in case of invasion. Alderman Bennett, as usual in his zeal and enthusiasm for his coun- try, had been rather too denunciatory, particu- larly as to the amount of financial support re- ceived. He said, for instance, with regard to the paucity of subscriptions, that it was a disgrace to everybody in Warrington. Surely, he did not mean everybody; for what of those who had subscribed? (Laughter.) Supposing an invad- ing army got as near as Sankey Bridges, he (the Deputy-Mayor) thought it would take a great many. training .corps ;to offer an effective repast- ance. (A Voice: "They would drop dead I." and another Voice: "Who? The Germans?" and laughter.) It must also be remembered that the public generally had been called upon to make many financial sacrifices, and that was all the more reason why they should be perfectly satisfied as to the utility. of the corps before sub- scribing to it. He had given the matter grave consideration, and he would prefer to defer judgment until replies had been received to the questions as to the probable duties of the mem- bers.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
UNPREPAREDNESS THE GREAT DRAWBACK.

Councillor ROBERTS opined that Alderman Bennett was not in his best form when he made certain more or less personal references, which did not strengthen his argument. It appeared to him that the only drawback to the success of their Army had been the unpreparedness of the large number of men who had sacrificed everything to become soldiers of the King and of the Empire, owing to the want of a system of training, which ought to have been in opera- tion years ago. The value of the home defence organisations would be in exact proportion to the amount of training they could get before

===== 0002601_19150605_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
The Burden of War Not Equally Borne.

He was not going to make any reflection upon WarringtOn. He was too proud of the town and of the people to do that. Warrington had responded, in men and in material, nobly dur- ing the great crisis, and Warrington was pre- pared to do still more. But they must not cOn- ceal from their minds the very unpleasant fact that the war did not lie equitably upon the shoulders of all of them. On some the burdens were very great—almost too great to be. borne; on others they rested very lightly indeed. The sel?-sacrifice evidenced by some people was a very slight matter. The resolution, if acted upon,. would draw into the net of contributors towards the expense of maintaining the Train- ing Corps in Warrington a very great many people who were escaping every contribution of every kind at the present time. Councillor Roberts went on to refer to the contemptuous way in which the Territorials were looked upon and treated for years. But where would the country have been to-day if it had not been for the magnificent Territorial Force, the great bulk of whom were serving abroad like soldiers and Englishmen, and who had gained laurels equal to any which the Regular •Army had obtained before? It was a matter for thanks to God that that force had been in operation and was in the fit state it had proved to be to take up its re- sponsible duties (Hear, hear.) "Don't let us smile at any man," said the Councillor, by way of conclusion, "no matter how old he is, if he is willing, at this crisis, ,to lift a spade or do anything else in order that England might be served. He should have our help and not our criticism." (Hear, hear.)

PRAISE FOR THE WARRINGTON CORPS.

The MAron said that the previous night he was talking to a gentleman who was reviewing the local Volunteer Training Corps—a man of experience—and he said, by way of compliment- ing the men, that he had seen other companies drill in other towns, and that the drill and general work of the Warrington men was much smarter than what he had been used to meeting with. The Training Corps consisted of some of the best citizens of Warrington—men who felt that it was their duty to make themselves fit for military work. Might not the Council very well encourage them to go on with their good work?

COUNCILLOR BROADHURST-CONVERT.

Councillor BROADHURST said he was one who opposed the proposal at the last meeting of the Council, but rsince then he had been thinking deeply about it. It was not because he had any doubt as to what the corps was capable of doing that be objected. If an invading force of- 1,200 was in the vicinity of Sankey Bridges, the mem- bers of the Volunteer Corps, with two months' training, could defend fieldworks to such pur- pose that it would be impossible for 12,000 Ger- mans to carry the town. The DEPUTY-MAYOR: What about 250,000 Ger- mans?

Councillor BROADHURST replied that in the event of an invasion on a large scale the whole of the training corps in the country would be organised into battalions and divisions. He was still of the opinion that tho; Government ought to provide for the maintenance and equip- ment of such corps, but, on the ground of choos- ing the lesser of two evils, he was prepared to support the giving of authority to municipalities to contribute to the cost of finding arms and ammunition.

COUNCILLOR COUGH THINKS IT WOULD

BE A WASTE OF MONEY.

Councillor GOUGH, in opposing the resolution, thought that the details which Alderman Ben- nett had given about the objects of the corps were insufficient. For instance, the Alderman had not told them who were the authorities of the corps and who would have the training power. -When one considered the seriousness of the war, he (Councillor (4ough) did not think they could trifle any longer with amateur train- ing corps. They wanted men of expert know- ledge to train such organisations. The MAYOR: We have got all that. Councillor Gotou : They want to be so organ- ised that they should be recognised. Alderman BENNETT : They are recognised. Councillor Goton : If tilly were recognised by the Government, I contend that the Govern- ment would not only make provision for their equipment, but would find the necessary funds. I do not think Alderman Bennett could have thought that this corps could face an invading army. If he considers the units that are re- quired to make up an army, this Corps would not be able to meet it. They are only old men who are going to be in it, and therefore, in my opinion, they are unsuitable for the work. I consider it would be a waste of money to vote for this. _ _ _

As stated abore, Alderman Bennett's pro- posals were adopted, there being only one dis- sentient voice.

Brewers' Appeal To The Public.

Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., Ltd., have circularised all the tenants of their licensed houses to the following effect: "We feel it our I duty to earnestly appeal to our tenants of licensed houses in these abnormal times to use increased vigilance in the management of their houses by the constant discouragement of exces- sive drinking, particularly by men in military uniform, who are unwittingly tempted by their numerous friends to drink more than is good for them. We are sure licence-holders will find it will ultimately be to their advantage to avoid anything that might give rise to the -elightest cause for complaint m

===== 0002601_19150605_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE COURTS.

IMPUDENT MENDIC•I\T.

Begging For " Doss-Money " With Over Two Shillings On Him.

Before the Borough magistrates on Monday, Joseph Nesbit, a stranger to the town, was charged with begging in Sankey-street on. Sat- urday night. A police officer stated that he received several complaints as to prisoner begging, and kept an eye on him. He was seen to stop several people, and the officer heard him ask for assistance to- wards a night's "doss." He said tkat he had walked eighteen miles and was tired out. When arrested he had 2s. 31d. in his possession. Prisoner said he had worked five hours that day for a local 'company, which he named, and he' was asking for money to help him to tide over the week-end, se that•he could start work again on the Monday' morning. He was sent to prison for fourteen days with hard labour.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
SANE ENOUGH!

But A Source Of Trouble To His Relatives.

A young man, of no fixed abode, nanied Thomas Conroy, appeared before the Borough magistrates on Monday to further answer a charge of having defrauded the L. and N.-W. RailwaY Company by travelling without a ticket from London to Warrington. He ad- mitted the offence.

The Chief Constable explained that the man had been remanded from the previous week, because some doubts existed in the minds of the police as to his sanity. But he appeared to be sane enough, though for long he had been a source of trouble to his relatives, who were're- spectable people. He had followed different vacations, hut had done no good in any of them. He was before the Court in July of last year on a charge of false pretences, and lie was then sent to prison for a month. Among other things he had been a collector, and his friends had paid money that he had done people out of, rather than that he "should bring further trouble on the family. He appeared to have been wandering about for some considerable time, and to have been "very much down on his luck." He had been given every chance, but it seemed impossible for him to go honestly. Prisoner: I made application to be allowed to sign my name so that I could pay for the ticket, hut they would not give me a chance to pay for it, but brought me down here instead. The Chief Constable: He had signed his name in a similar way several times, but none of the fares have been forthcoming. Prisoner was fined 255., with the Alternative of 21 days' impzisonment.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
"AS REGULAR AS CLOCKWORK."

Last lance For A Police Court Habitue.

"If he is not in prison, he comes up here regularly once a month," remarked the Chief Constable at Monday's Police Court, when Alphonse Sculland was put in the dock to fur- ther answer a charge of haying been drunk and disorderly in Barbauld-street, a charge to which, at a previous court, he had pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable told the Bench that when the man was before the Court last week application was made for an adjournment, in order that the police might have the opportu- nity of making inquiries with a view to getting the man into an inebriates' home for a term of three years. With the -consent of the Bench, however, he (the Chief Constable) was desirous of giving the man one moye ohance, on condition that he was sentenced to the full term of im- prisonment for the charge for which he was brought up, and that he should be bound over to be of good behaviour in the future, finding two sureties, or, in default, going to gaol for two months. In the alternative he (the Chief) asked that the man be 'sent to an inebriates' home for three years, He had been before the Court no fewer than .7.3 ,times before this; in- deed, he was as regular as clockwork in his appearances, and his record covered many years. The Bench acquiesced in the Chief Constable's suggestion.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
ESCORT "ON THE RAZZLE."

Drunk At Bank Quay Station And

Is Arrested.

John Guest, of the R.P.A., was charged at Monday's Police Court—before Mr. S. Wright (in the chair), Alderman Pierpoint, Bolton and Smethurst, and Messrs. W. Roberts, G. Pilling, W. Furness and T. Locker—with har- ing been drunk in the Public-hall at Bank Quay (L. and N.-W.R.) Station on Saturday, an offence to which he pleaded guilty. _ _ _ _

The evidence was to the effec-t that shortly .after 2 a.m. on Saturday the attention of a police officer was called to prisoner, who was in the booking hall of the station and was in a drunken state and was trying to get past the barrier. He said he formed part of an escort that had been to Manchester under the charge of a bombardier. He had paid his fare to War- rington and wanted to get back to rejoin the bombardier and the remainder of the escort. He added that he had neither ticket nor pass. When told that the train he ought to have caught had gone, he said he didn't care whether he went back or not; and if the mili- tary authorities wanted him they would have to come and fetch him.

• He was remanded pending instructions from his C.O.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
TWELVE-YEAR-OLD SHOP-LIFTER.

A Day Out On "A Proper Pilfering Expedition."

A twelve-year-old girl, very small for her age, stood before the magistrates at a Chil- dren's Court held on Monday, and glibly ad- mitted thefts from Messrs. Woolworth's Bazaar and the boot department of the Co- operative Stores, and she and her father were bound over.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
Stole Toys From Bazaar Stalls.

The Chief Con-stable said that on Saturday the girl was seen to go up to a stall in Messrs. Woolworth's bazaar and take some celluloid toys and put them into a bag. She then went to an adjoining stall and took some rubber balls, which she also put into her bag. An attendant thereupon made for her, but she ran away. She was chased and captured, and her bag was found to be practically full of stolen property, the articles in it including two pairs of boots, which it was subsequently discovered were stolen from the Co-operative Stores.. The girl, however, told another story concerning the boots, viz., that she had taken them from a handcart standing outside Arpley Station. The truth appeared to be that she went into the Co-operative Stores with a sister, who re- quired a pair of boots. Whilst the sister was waiting to be fitted the prisoner purloined the two pairs of boots found in her bag. Apart from her stealing proclivities, the girl ap- peared to be very untruthful. Her father, who lived at Walton, appeared to be a highly respectable man. He lost his wife some six years ago, and was left with five or six chil. dren to bring up unaided. A plucky little girl of fifteen years of age had charge of her brothers and sisters, and she appeared to do wonderfully well, for when the police visited the house they found it very well kept, indeed.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
Why She Took The Articles.

The girl told the Bench that she took the toys because she thought they would do for her sisters. As to the boots, she said that she was going to give one pair to a sister, and, when they were worn out, she was going to give her the other pair. The Chief Constable said that the prisoner also had in her possession a pair of cotton gloves, which she had stolen caviler from a

===== 0002601_19150605_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
shop at Lower Walton; in fact, she appeared to have been out on a proper pilfering expedi- tion that day.

The girl iaid she did not take the gloves from Lower. Walton, but from a stall in the Market.

The Chief Constable said that was, so far as the police could discover, quite untrue. She went into a shop at Walton to buy some rib- bon remnants, and whilst she was being served with these she stole the gloves. The police could not find that she had done pilfering of this sort before.

The girl promised never to steal again.

HOW THEY GET DRUNK.

Drink Whisky In The Streets When

" Pubs." Are Shut.

When a man was before the Borough Police Court on Monday morning, charged with har- ing been drunk at a late hour on Saturday night, the\Chief Constable said that there had been a lot of late drunkenness during the past few weeks, since the Order for earlier closing of public-houses came-into force. Having had as much as they could comfortably carry up to "throwing-out time," numbers fof men were in the habit of taking out bottles of whisky with them and drinking it in the streets, with the result that they frequently got into a beastly' condition of drunkenness. The pri- soner in this case was found lying in the street helplessly drunk at 11.20 at night, nearly an hour and a half after the public-houses had closed.

The defendant, who said that he took the whisky because he was feeling ill, was fined:

"IF ' COPPER ' COMES UP I'LL CUT

===== 0002601_19150605_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS THROAT."

Drunken Man's Foolish Threat.

In a rich brogue, which the Chief Constable explained to Tuesday's Bench, was "Glasgow Irish," John Riley, a labourer of no settled abode, explained that it was force of circum- stances which was to blame for his appearance in the dock to answer a charge of having been drunk and disorderly at 10.50 the previous night, for, he said, had he been able to get into his "lodge," it would have been unnecessary for, him to utter the fulminations anent blank- dash landlords which he felt constrained to utter at finding himself locked out, and which a policeman, unhappily for him, overheard, and on which he based the charge. P.C. Macdonald said he saw prisoner and another man talking outside a common lodg- ing-house. First they talked of the landlord and then they referred to the police, and, anent the latter, the Glasgow-Irishman observed: "If a 'copper' comes up I'll cut his throat. The ' coppers ' in this town are no good; they ought .to all be at the Front." Thereupon witness stepped forward, but, instead of cutting his throat, the man grew apologetic, observing: "I didn't mean you!" and blaming the dero- gatory references on to his companion, who, however, was, by comparison, sober, and who was quite quiet.

Pris-oner, who was before the Court so re- cently as May 11th, was fined 9s. 6(1., with the alternative otfourteen days' iniprisonment.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A CANDID PRISONER.

Said He Had Spent Most Of His Time

In Prison.

`` He said to the officer. that he had Emit most of his time in prison,. and I should think that is about the most truthful remark he has ever made." These words were uttered at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday morning by the Chief Constable (Mr. Martin Nicholls) in referring to the charge of drunkenness and disorderly behaviour preferred against Wil- liam Bate, a youngish man, of no settled abode. The evidence was to the effect that the man was seen by a police officer to be in a drunken state, going from public-house to public-house. The officer followed him in and cautioned the landlords against serving him, a procedure which caused the prisoner to grow abusive, and to utter the remark referred to by the Chief Constable.

Prisoner, who was certainly of military bearing, said that he had been recently dis- charged from the Army on account of rheuma- tism. He admitted that he had been into a public-house with some soldiers and "had had a drink or two." Up to the previous night he had been working on a timber boat and he should have re-started at eight o'clock that morning. A fine of 76. Gd. was imposed.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
DOMESTIC FELICITY (?).

Wife Says Husband Is Drunkard; He Says She Is Bad-Tempered.

Before Messrs. H. Roberts (in the chair), C. Jackson Holmes and T. Locker, at Tuesday's Police Court, Daniel OuDram, of 13, Mill-street, was summoned by his wife for assault, the hear- ing of the case occupying the best part of an hour and a half.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
A Bank Holiday Episode.

Mr. P. S. Steel, for the plaintiff, said the de- fendant was employed as a blacksmith, whilst his wife kept a stall in the Market, and also took in lodgers. The assault was committed on Whit Monday, on the afternoon of which day defendant came home drunk and started quar- relling about a sofa, which he had smashed up and burned the previous day, because one of the lodger's children had been sitting on it. He then got some bacon from the meat safe, put it into a frying pan and then turned it on to the fire. His wife remonstrated, and he stabbed her in the little finger with a fork and subse- quently struck her when she followed him into the backyard. Somewhere between 10 and 11 o'clock the same night defendant struck his wife in the mouth with his fist and knocked a tooth out, whilst he also picked up the top of ,an iron bedrail and hit her about the body with it. He also threatened to throw her down- stairs and break her neck, and as they were at the head of the staircase at the time the wife sank to her knees and clung to the man, the result being that in the ensuing struggle they both tumbled down the steps together. Since the assault defendant had several times re- quested his wife to withdraw the summons, but she felt that she could not do so. The lodger and his wife were so terrified at defendant's conduct that they dare not go up to their bed- room.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
" Some " Thirst.

In her evidence bearing out her advocate's opening statement, the wife said that when her husband woke up on Whit Monday night, after partly sleeping off the effects of the strong drink he had had earlier in the day, he drank seven bottles of lemon squash, a pint of beer and a pint of cold water. She added that he was nothing else but a drunkard, and volubly de- clared to the Bench that neither they nor all the solicitors living in the town, would make her live with her husband any longer. She had made her mind up, and no matter what the decision was that day, she had "finished with him for good and all."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
PERSISTENT THIEF'S PLEA.

Told Bench He Wanted Chance To

Start Afresh.

"You are a -persistent thief," said the pre- siding magistrate at Wednesday's Borough Police Court, to a smart-looking, well-dressed man giving the name of Edward Frood, who was stated to have a number of aliases, and who was charged. with stealing on April Bth, 1913, from. a. shop in Horsemarket-street, War- rington, a Motor cycle, valued at £l5, the pro- perty of Frederick Charles Dutton.—He ad- mitted the offence.

A Two-year-old Theft.

'The Chief Constable explained that in April, two years ago, Mr. Dutton, who is an agricul- tural implement dealer, sent his motor bicycle to Messrs. Bellian and Davies's to he repaired. It was duly repaired and returned to Mr. Dutton. At that time prisoner was employed by Messrs. Bellian and Davies; he had been with them about a fortnight, passing under the name of Johnson. After the bicycle had been returned to Mr. Dutton prisoner rang him .up on the telephone and asked him if it had been satisfactorily repaired. Mr. Dutton re- plied that it was still not altogether satisfactory and prisoner said :." I'll take it and put it in order." He.duly fetched the cycle away, but, instead of taking it to his employers' shop, he decamped with it and drove it to a shop in Salford, where he disposed of it for £s—re- ceiving ..,£"2 on account and being promised a further £3.

Prisoner said had the charge been brought against him at his trial at the West Riding Quarter Sessions he would have said "Yes" to it. He hoped the Bench would make the sentence run concurrently with the term he was at present serving, and which, be was given to understand, was in expiation of all his offences. Several Previous Convictions.

The Chief Constable said that before his trial at the Sessions the prisoner had the opportunity of admitting this offence along with the other, and of having it taken into account in the sentence which was passed upon him, but when the charge was brought to his notice, whilst he was in prison awaiting trial, he denied all knowledge of Mr. Dutton's mathine. The prisoner was a native of Man- chester and was born in 1885. Since 1909 he had been sentenced to several short terms of imprisonment for theft and false pretences. Prisoner told the Bench that he was sorry for what' he had done and he wanted to have the opportunity of making a fresh start. He was ordered six months' imprisonment, with hard labour, to follow at the close of the sentence which he is at present serving in Wakefield Gaol.

TICKETLESS TRAVELLER.

Said He Had I.lSted And Is Remanded

For Inquiries..

James Rice, whose address Was not stated, was charged, at Wednesday's Police Court, with travelling on the London and North- Western Railway from Preston to Warrington without a ticket and without haring previously paid his fare, an offence which he admitted. A police-constable stated that at 10 p.m. .on Tuesday night he was in Parker-street, when he heard a row at Bank Quay Station, and upon .inquiring the cause found that prisoner had alighted from a train ticketless and was talking excitedly to the ticket collector. He took him into custody. Prisoner's defence was that he had enlisted and that he was drunk at the time he com- mitted the offence. The Chief Constable said there was reason to believe that the man had enlisted at the Pub- lic Hall, Preston, and that he was on his way to Birr, in Ireland, to join the Le,insters. He asked that the case might be adjourned for three days in order to make sure that the man's statement as to enlisting was absolutely correct. If it were found to be untrue he would be brought up again.

MATRIMONIAL DIFFERENCES.

liu,,i)and's Jealousy Said To 11-zle At

Bottom Of Trouble.

Before Mr. S. Wright and other magistrates, .at the Borough Police Court yesterday morning, Wm. Henry Talbot, of 21, Eldon-street, was summoned by his wife, Elizabeth Alice Talbot. for persistent cruelty, wherefore she asked for a separation order.—He denied cruelty. The wife said that the husband "made her black and blue," and strongly objected to her going out, alleging "other men" as the reason. .She was the mother of -thirteen children, and two of her sons were serving in the Army. She was married twenty-three years ago. Inspector Wainwright, of the N.S.P.C.C., said the wife had complained to him about her hus- band ill-using her, and he had seen bruises on her arm. Apparently the man drank too much and was jealous of his wife. He expected her to be in at 9 o'clock at night, and if she was not in at that hour he accused her of being up to no good. The husband said all the bother arose because he demanded to know where his wife had been when she came home at about midnight. As to making her "black and blue," such bruises as she got were received when he tried to stop her from hitting him with a poker. It was her own fault; she had got a very had temper. The Chief Constable said the man was fined for being drunk and disorderly only the previous day. The case was adjourned for a month.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE MILK SUPPLY.

Women to Assist on Farms to Remove Danger.

Miss Knowles, the senior organising officer for women's work under the Board of Trade, speaking on Tuesday at a Birkenhead work for women meeting, appealed to the women to help in this crisis. A large number of em- ployers, she said, had agreed to engage women in the manufacture of munitions, and the re- sponse had been 'very good. up to now. The question of women labour in the agricultural industry was receiving attention. Women were especially needed in this direction, parti- cularly in Cheshire, where, consequent upon so many farm hands enlisting, farmers were selling their cows. The milk supply was, therefore, in danger. Women could also help by undertaking clerical work. 'Miss Knowles urged women to register and hold themselves in readiness to take up duties.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
KRUPP'S BOAST.

Giant Ingots By British Process.

"I am sorry to have to confess it, but shortly .before the war I was dining with the managing director of Krupps, who told me that they were making steel ingots, weighing 1-10 tons, for guns, by the crucible process evolved 175 years ago by an Englishman, Ben- jamin Huntsman," said Professor John Oliver Arnold, of Sheffield University, at the Royal Institution on Tuesday. Neither the Germans nor the Americans, however tproceeded the professor), could pro- duce the Sheffield white crucible. That was a secret handed down from father to KG in Sheffield. In the North of England they were making ingots weighing 150 tons, and there were 12,000- ton presses squeezing out armour plate like cheese. Professor Arnold went on to tell of what he described as a tragedy in the history of metal- lurgy. M. Martin, who originated the "open hearth method," was imitated by others and eventually disappeared. He was found in 1910 in the direst poverty. French and British metallurgists had now placed M. Maitin in comfortable circumstances for the rest of his life, and he had been awarded the highest honour—the Bessemer Gold Medal.
