THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY. JUNE 5, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR NEW BARONET.

Warrington has been honoured this week by His IN.U.TEsTr, who has conferred upon Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, one of her best and most esteemed sons, the dignity of a baronetcy. SIR. ARTHUR CROSFIELD, as we shall now know the ex-member for the borough, has well earned the distinction, and the town, which has played its part in helping along the general progress Of the country, was due some mark of official recognition. It was fitting that this gracious mark of the KING'S interest in our town Should have taken the form of conferring a title upon one who had through a long and arduous period of service largely contributed to the building up of the great firm of Messrs. J. CROSFIELD and SONS; who had evinced a practical and fruitful interest in the social life of our Working people; and who, in addition to his local achievements, was closely connected with the higher walks of policy and diplomacy, and was persona grata in the highest political circles; and who, perhaps most weighty consideration of all, has set himself the task of bringing the nations together to solve their labour problems in _unison, and so " to civilise- the conditions of industry." SIR ARTHUR CROSFIELD is a far- seeing man, and was shrewd enough to per- ceiNle that his pre-eminence in certain classes of sport could be pressed into useful service on the Continent. Rubbing shoulders with similarly placed men from all .parts of Europe and America, he astutely made the close inter- course with them that rules among sports- men the occasion for furthering his ideals on internationalism, which May well account for the 'cOMmanding position 'he holds in the Councils of the International Association for Labour Legislation, whose work seems destined to bring about the most beneficent results.

While we are congratulating SIR ARTRIIR in the name of the town, as we are sure we may claim the privilege of doing, we must also con- gratulate LADY CROSFIELD, upon whom the honourable title that she will share with her devoted husband will sit with especial appropriateness. Her Ladyship ever proved herself to be one of Nature's real gentle- women—she was the gold to which a gracious SOVEREIGN has now added the guinea stamp.

In the list of honours by which MB Majesty the King- marked his fiftieth , birthday On Thursday was the following name among six gentlemen upon whom baronetcies_ had been conferred:—

===== 0002601_19150605_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
LORD DERBY, RECRUITER!

LORD DERBY is about to open a recruiting campaign at Warrington, in which his band, the Band of the Scottish Pipers, and the Band of the 4th South Lancashires will take a prominent part. The bands will arrive at the Central Station next Friday at 5.55 and parade the town, and meetings will be held at various points en route. A van cinema will show pictures. At about eight o'clock a big Meeting wilk be held, probably at Bridge

On the Saturday a monster meeting will be tield in Front of the Town Hall, or near the imidstand in the Park, and the bands will play a kind of military tattoo. Mr. W. T. WILSON,. Labour M.P., will be the principal ipeaker, and a splendid programme of music lescriptive of camp life, battle and victory will be. given by the recruiting band, under Bandmaster A. E. HALFORD.

On the following Monday afternoon a visit will be paid to the outlying districts, and an appeal is made to patriotic owners of cars to lend 30 cars to carry four each, from 2.30 to 9 p.m,, starting from the Drill Hall at the former time. Lieut. C. L. WILLIAmsos, Drill Hall, will be pleased to receive the names of those who will kindly oblige in the matter of cars. It is sought to make the campaign a success, and we hope all who can will help.

ON GOING STEADILY.

Sale 01 Work At Golborne-street

Baptist Church.

With the object of reducing a debt of .21,000 a sale of work was held at the Golloorne-street Baptist Church yesterday and the day before. The opening on Thursday was performed by Mrs. C. E. Parker, Mrs. E. Osborne presiding. The Rev. H. Ogden (pastor) alluded to the fact that £1,5e0 ef the £2,500 debt had already been cleared off, and testified to the excellent work that had been put in in order to achieve so much. The outstanding debt of a thousand pounds that they were now attempting- to de- crease represented the cost of the Sunday School premises. The pastor also mentioned that thirty of the men folk connected with the church had joined the colours; which he thought was not bad for' a chutch of the size of theirs.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR ARTHUR AND LADY CROSFIELD.

Mrs. Osborne, in the course of her remarks, wished the sale every success, and expressed herself as always willing to help in any good work of the kind that had brought that assembly together. Referring to the honour conferred by the King on Mr: A. H. Crosfield, Mrs. Osborne said that Mr. Crosfield—now Sir Arthur Cros- field, Bart.—was known to every man, woman, boy and girl in Warrington. No name was more respected in the good old town 'that that of Cros- field. ,In honouring Mr. Crosfield, the King honoured his citizens in Warrington. She felt sure- that they all joined in the heartiest con- gratulations to Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150605_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
BEFORE THE WAR WAS THOUGHT OF.

Mrs. C. E. Parker made an excellent opening speech. In these times, she said, there was a great necessity for our going steadily. We were going steadily; England was noted for it. She was repeatedly told by the Belgians, with whom we had become quite familiar, that what struck them most about the English people was their kindneiti and their almost unfailing calm-. ness. We had got to continue to support these things, to which we were pledged before ever the war was thought of. One of .the best things we 'could do was to continue to keep church affairs alive. •

Having wished the, effort every success and declared the. Sale open, Mrs. Parker was Pre- sented by Miss Hilda Stevens with a bouquet. Miss Olive Balmford was the medium of a similar compliment to ,Mrs. Osborne. A vote of thanks was accorded to the two ladies,, on the proposition of the Rev. T. T. James, of the Wycliffe Congregational Church, seconded by Mr. Owen, one of the deacons of the Golborne-street Chursh.

Business then commenced at the various well- laden stalls, which were Ataffed principally by ladies of. the congregation. _

Inclqding the iift-s and donations, the tota realised on the first day was £ll3. The sale was continued last night.

SIR ARTHUR CROSFIELD, BART., AND LADY CROSFIELD.

His Majesty The King's Recognition Of Valuable Services.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
ARTHUR 'HENRY CROSFIELD. Es()

.Warringtonians, without distinction of class, sect, or polities, have openly rejoiced at this mark of favour which the King has graciously shown to the t6wn through a worthy .representative of • a family that for a

century has striven worthily, and, to use one of Shakespeare's words of strongest emphasis, mightily, for the moral, religious, educational and industrial well-being of the town of their adoption, and, consequently, for the per- manent 'good. of the country of • Which War- ringtoil is ever growing a more important

tiOnal Co-operation and Internationalism ap- pealed to him, and shortly after entering the Parliament of 1906 he joined the-International. Association for Labour Legislation. He has ,done more towards the support of the British section of that Association, financially and by personal service, than any other one man. The set policy of this excellent Association is to civilise the conditonS of. industry, a hopeless task if the manufacturers and workers of the several competing countries remain isolated. The latest effort of Sir Arthur and his friends has been toinstitute an eight-hour shift at the blast furnaces, in what are called continuous industries, in all , countries, and readers of "The Examiner" will know :how strenuously Sir Arthur has appealed to the English Home Office to give a lead to the world in this matter. Two significant actual achievements of the Association may be mentioned to indicate its scope And objects. It is solely owing to the efforts of the Association, and to the interna- tional co-operation it has secured among other nations, that the dreadful disease, phosphorous

===== 0002601_19150605_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR ARTHUR CROSFIELD, BART.

part. ~What Warrington would have been. . , without the fiiisfields, how much poorer the Place Would be without the present head of the family now so fittingly recognised by his SOvereign, may be realised only if we carefully try to imagine what would be the condition of things had the activities which we shall proceed to place on record been absent. This honour has come as a recognition of a century of hard and consistent endeavours in all the great progressive causes which Mr. Arthur H. Crosfield—now so happily Sir Arthur H. Crosfield, Baronet—has faithfully laboured to perpetuate. Sir Arthur's interests have not by any means been confined by local boundaries. Very early in life—he was the son of the late Mr. John Crosfield, of Walton Lea, Warrington— following the traditions of the Crosfield family, he took an earnest and intelligent interest in national affairs. Later he was fortunate in being able to extend his know- ledge of the world, and especially of the countries of Europe, by extensive travel. In this respect he has occupied a unique posi- tion and his efforts to ameliorate the conditions of labour—to help to civilise the conditions of industry, as he has before now expressed it— are bound to have borne rich fruit, though the full harvest has been rudely delayed by tha outbreak of war. As an ardent lover of peace he did all within his power to promote a good understanding between our own and Contin- ental countries; but his intimacy with Con- tinental thought and feeling, and his insight which detected the ambitions of Germany, made him a strong advocate of adequate pre- parations for defence, particularly of a Naval character. Possibly some of his political friends were ef the opinion that he too often emphasised the need of such preparations, but events have proved that he was right. We , may be sure that ,Sir Arthur Crosfield's ser- vices in an 'international, no less than in a national and local sense, played ,an appro- priate part in the winning of this substantial honour.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
AN OLD AND GENEROUS FAMILY.

The new baronet comes of old and highly- esteemed stock. The Crosfields have dwelt in the 'Lake District from 1530 down to the pre- sent time, though branches have spread to various parts of the country, Sir Arthur being the head. of the Warrington branch, which has a history of just a hundred years. A few years after. George Fox commenced to preach, the Crosfields, always aligh& respected middle 'class family, joined the 'Society of Friends; and they have been predominantly a Quaker family from then almost down to the present time.. • • In 1814 Joseph Crosfield, Sir Arthur's grand- father;: left the Lake District and came to Warrington to make preparations for com- mencing his business career .here, and the centenary of that historic migration was cele- brated last July at the opening of the fine Memorial Hall erected to the memory of the 'late Mr. John Crosfield (Sir Arthur's .father) by his widow, Mrs. John Crosfield. .Mr. Joseph Crosfield founded the business in 1815, the business which has. now grown into the vat concern of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, which this year attains its centenary. Warrington Town Hall and Park was secured largely through the generosity. of the late. Mr. George Crosfield (Sir Arthur's uncle), who gave £9,500 to start the fund to effect the purchase; the late Mr. John Crosfield (Sir Arthur's father), who was given the freedom of the. borough in recognition of his interest in public education, gave us our School of Art. For the Town Clock, the Conservatory in. the Park, mission halls, and other valuable gifts we are indebted to the beneficence that has flowed from the same family.. Sir Arthur has in this, as in other matters, followed the practice of his ancestors with open-handed freedom. When the war broke out he was engaged upon the work of reconstructing a house in the North of London,. his town resi- dence of the future. It is not generally known, but the work upon the house was stopped by Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield, and the money which' had been allocated for that

===== 0002601_19150605_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
DELIGHTED TOWN.

work was diverted to. the various war funds, which have been so liberally supported. Not only money but time has been devoted to war purposes, both. Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield having given practically the whole of their time to assist the various voluntary • efforts which have done .so much g6id in many direc- tions. This really fine example of self-sacri- fice in the present time of national stress is characteristic of a Crosfield and might well .be taken as a pattern by many others, be their resources greater or less,, than the subject of this article.

Of course, giving is not everything; and the real affection which the people of Warrington have had for three generations of the faintly is proof that their hearts, as well as their purses, have been at the disposal of the people amongst whoM they have lived and lahoi:

SIR ARTHUR'S COMMERCIAL RECORD

The husine§s of Messrs. josepll croslield, I soap manufacturers, was easily first ht the

===== 0002601_19150605_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
LADY CR,OSFIELD. (From a recent sketch by Sargeant.)

trade in the middle of the nineteenth century, but by the early 'Eighties it had, for various reasons, begun to decline. Master Arthur went into the business in 1882, at a very early age, literally "took Off his coat," went 'through the practical work of a soap maker, and, up to the year 1896, devoted almost the whole of his time and energy and powers of initiation to the task of placing the business in its old commanding position. His unceasing efforts over a long period were undoubtedly largely responsible for replacing the business once again on a sure foundation and upon the way that led to its present-day greatness. He acquired a complete mastery, not only ,of the large questions of policy and management, but of the whole detail of those branches of trade with which he was concerned; and in less than four years after 1884, when his father gave him, at the age of nineteen, the chief authority and with it the heaviest weight of responsibility in the concern, he was elected honorary secretary for one of the most im- portant trade associations in the country, a position he held throughout the whole of his active business career.'

The practical achievements of this Assobia- tion, so far as they go, have been invaluable. That its work should for the time being have been interrupted owing to this fearful European and world-wide conflagration is one further piece of evidence in proof a the measureless and hideous crime of which those responsible for it are guilty.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
SOCIAL PLEASURES FOR WORKERS.

It was during the time that Sir Arthur practically controlled the business—he was fourteen years a director and afterwards seven years chairman of the company—that the re- markable social side of the firm was instituted and developed. He took a very active part in inaugurating and then developing that social work, and had the unbounded satisfaction of seeing a system of recreation carried into effect, under the advice and with the financial assist- ance of the firm, that has been the admiration of the industrial world and has found not alew imitators among enlightened employers every- where. Grounds for football, cricket, tennis, hockey, bowling, and all healthy games for men and 'women were acquired and largely sup- ported by the employes. *The intellectual, as well as physical, forms of recreation were given every encouragement. Always fond of music himself, Sir Arthur fostered its development among the staff. As a result the Crosfield Per- fection Band, which has eventually achieved a world-wide reputation, came into existence. He presented the band with its first complete set of instruments, and, with the firm, co-operated in getting the necessary instruction for the players, and has kept in personal touch with its leaders for years. Crosfield's Band became one of the "prize bands" of the country, and has carried off the highest prize at the national competitions at the Crystal Palace. Then the employes have their Operatic and Dramatic Society, which stages an opera, provides the cast, the orchestra, and indeed all that is needful, in a mAnner that places it high in ithe ranks of amateur societies of the kind. The firm also provide the studiously minded with scientific instruction free of charge, as they also encourage music by arranging for any of the female staff so inclined to receive free of charge instruction in piano playing or in playing the violin. The staff are also provided with a fine assembly room, where dances are given for their enjoyment.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
IN POLITICS.

For the past thirty-five years Sir Arthur Cros- field has been in the forefront in Warrington politics, always an assiduous and sincere cham- -1)ion of Liberalism and Progress. His cousin William, contested Wa4ington and lost in 1885, and .his father fought the same battle in 1886 and lost. It was not until 1900 that Sir Arthur appeared upon the scene as candidate—and lost, but that was in what was known as the khaki election. He stuck to his guns and had the satisfaction of forming, by virtue of a record Imajority for the constituency, one of the great phalanx of Liberals that in 1906 placed Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman in power .and gave the Liberals a start upon that remarkable period of power which only the intervention of the war nine Years later has interrupted. In January, 1910, he held the seat by a reducied majority, and in December lest it by 246 votes. He is still the standard-bearer of local Liberalism.

INTERNATIONAL POLITICS.

Many years ago the i mportance of Interim-

===== 0002601_19150605_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
SPORT.

Sir Arthur Crosfield's favourite out-door sport has been golf, at which he has achieved some degree of efficiency. In 1905 he won the French Amateur Championship, and he was four times Amateur Champion of the Engadine in Switzer- land, where for many years past he has spent his summer holidays. He was, in fact, one of the pioneers of the game in that part of the world, and some fifteen years ago, when the Swiss Golf Association was formed, he was invited to be- come the first president. This position he ac- cepted. He has also been Captain of the Can- nes Golf Club' for some ten years—where the golf is as distinctly cosmopolitan as any other club in existence.

Sir Arthur always used the opportunitiee afforded by meeting men of all nations in field sports to further the objects of that sane internationalism in which he so firmly be- lieves. He was the pioneer of bowling on a large scale by the support he gave to the Cemetery Inn Club 21 years ago.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
HELP FOR WAR FUNDS.

We have already referred to the fact that Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield stopped the work upon their new home, in, London BO that they might have the more available resources with Which to contribute towards, the funds being raised and the work being done for our soldiers and sailors. They supplied wool to anyone in Warrington who would knit gloves and mittens, and the women folk connected with' most of the works in town took advan- tage of the offer with the most gratifying re- suits. They worked and gave—and are work- ing and giving—most lavishly to the National funds and the local funds in Warrington for the relief of the Belgian refugees, for the provision of equipment of our Warrington Territorials, and the hospitals in Warrington. Sir Arthur has attended recruiting meetings in the town, journeyed to the camps or the Territorials while they were in training, and since their departure has provided them with socks and mittens, periscopes and mirrors, dried fruits and substantial quantities of food and comforts of all kinds to help to keep them in health and the good spirits so essen.; tial to enable them ,to achieve the victory that is to be their reward. THE LATEST CROSFIELD GIFT.

A posthumous marble bust of the late Mr. John Crosfield, which is' to be placed in the Town Hall, is now complete. The work has been done under the constant supervision of Mrs. John Crosfield and other members of the family have kept in touch with Mr. Pegram, the gifted sculptor, with the result that a life-like bust has been produced which will recall Mr. John Crosfield to Warringtonians as they knew him in the full vigour of middle life. The bust has been subscribed for by members of the 'family and nearest relatives, land iSir Arthur has commissioned Mr. Pegram to, make him a bronze bust exactly similar in character to the marble 'bust. This copy will be placed in the John Crosfield Memorial Hall.

SIR ARTHUR AND ‘4 THE EXAMINER."

Just as Mr. John Crosfield played an active part in helping to start "The Examiner,", in order that progressive thought and Liberal ideals might be fittingly propagated, 'so his son has recently taken, in conjunction with Mr. Charles Parker, an activeand prominent part in strengthening the newspaper and in. widely extending, under the Present manage- ment, its circulation and influence—an influ- enc4 for good not only in Warrington, but now, we are proud to 'add, in surrounding constitu- encies .

===== 0002601_19150605_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
LADY CROSFIELD,

‘Vhose Charming Personality Will

Grace The Title.

Sir Arthur and Lady Crosfield were married at the end of 1907, when the former was M.P. for Warrington. The bride, in wholn all Warringtou took a profound interest, was Miss Domini Elliadi, daughter of Mr. E. M. Elliadi, the son of Greek parents, who, since 1871, has been a naturalised British subject. Lady Cros- field's mother's maiden name was Homer, and she is a member of a very old Greek family, which claims, to be descended from the great Greek poet. Lady Crosfield was born at Southport and educated in Smyrna and in Paris. Lady Crosfield has so many accomplish- ments in which she excels that it is difficult to place them in order of merit. She is an excellent linguist and speaks many languages really well. Taking, as she does, a deep interest in her husband's political life and being a fluent speaker, with a plentiful com- mand of the saving grace of humour, she makes a most successful and winning plat- form speaker, and a successful president of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association. She is a , most brilliant pianist, and has thrilled large audiences on those occasions, which have not ,been infrequent, when she has given her services to assist charitable objects: Only recently she, with her husband, gave a con-

cert in Parr Hall, when record takings were devoted to the Belgian Relief Fund. Lady Crosfield is a keen sportswoman an.a as a Continental prize-winner at lawn tenno she is said to have few equals. She is a charming hostess, and whether in London or Warrington is a universal favourite.

LOCAL INTELLIGENCE.

OUR SOLDIER BOYS

Wish For A Good Supply

Of Hozne News.

From every nook and corner of the vast area of the world into which, the war carries British soldiers and sailors, "The Examiner" is constantly receiving words of cheer and commendation.

"The Examiner." gives all the home news, military and domestic, and the finest pictures.

Our readers should make a point of sending "The Examiner" to their friends and relatives at the Front, on the sea, in the training camps, and, last but not least, in the hospitals.

News from home is life to most of the men away from home. If readers'would like us to save them trouble we will post the paper weekly to any address in the United Kingdom for lid. per copy, or abroad for 2d. per copy.

A Trifle Warmer.

The mean temperature in Warrington during the week ended May 29 was 55 deg., or one degree higher than in the preceding week. The warmest day last week was Thursday, when a maximum temperature of 77 was recorded, whilst the coldest day was Saturday, when the glass fell to 34 deg.

Mr. Robert Pierpoint.

To the June issue of the "The Dickensian," Mr. Robert Pierpoint, ex-member for Warring- ton, contributes another striking article upon "Dickens and Public Executions." Of this the chief value is that it shows how a letter from the sgreat novelist to "The Times" was re- printed in a contemporary volume, and may therefore be included in Dickensian bibliology— of which apparently it has not hitherto formed a part.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
New Photographic Process'.

" Bromoil " was the subject of a demonstra- tion, given to members of the Warrington Photo- graphic Society, under the auspices of the Lancashire and Cheshire Photographic Union by Mr. S. H Williams, of Leigh, on Tuesday even- ing. Bromoil is a process by which the picture is produced in ordinary printing ink as distinct from the usual methods, in which the picture is produced by what is known as the silver process. Having thoroughly explained the whole process, the lecturer induced the mem- bers present to try their hands at making Bromoil pictures, and much surprise was ex- pressed at the simplicity of the process.—The thanks of the meeting were tendered to the lec- turer for a most instructive demonstration.

A Sixpenny War Bonus!

The minutes of the Museum Committee, pre- sented for confirmation at the monthly meeting of the Warriniton Town Council on Tuesday evening, contained a resolution to the effect that a war bonus of 6d. per week be paid to Mrs. Rimmer, one of the employes.—Councillor Broadhurst: It is to be hoped she won't make a beast of herself on 6d. a week. (Laughter.) The committee ought to' be ashamed of voting such a miserable pittance to anyone. If I could not have made the amount a shilling I would have left it alone.—Councillor Jenkins: It will at least provide her with a pinch of 'snuff, if she takes it. (More laughter.)—Alderman Bennett the chairman of/the committee in question, said he would withdraw the resolution in reference to the bonus.

Too Young to be Adopted.

A kind-hearted individual has applied to the Warrington Guardians for permission .tb adept the unfortunate infant wh6 came into; the cus- tody of the Poor Law under the strange cir- cumstances detailed in a previous issue. Readers will recollect that the baby wais handed to a girl by a woman who asked her to hold the child while she (the supposed mother) went into a tavern for a quick refreshment, and that when the baby-leaver did not turn up to claim the "dumped goods" the dismayed damsel turned the treasitre over to another girl, who, in turn, got rid of the bundle of responsibility by taking it to the Workhouse. A reward of .P.. 5 was offered by the Guardians for information as to the child's parents, but no news has been forthcoming. The application of the would-be festee-parents wag not granted, the Board hold- ing that the baby was too young to be adopted.

TERRITORIAL ROLL OF HONOUR.

A FORMIDABLE OFFICIAL LIST OF CASUALTIES

TOTAL 160.

The fourth official list of casualties in the ranks of the 4th South Lancashires was re- eeived on Tuesday this week by courtesy of Lieutenant Williamson, in command at the brill Hall. Five of those notified' as killed and seven Wounded had already been dealt with by us, ofacialry, while one now reported to have already wed from wounds, sustained May 9th, had been counted in .our list of wounded. These facts have to be borne in mind when the totals are being considered. The new narnes to be added to The Roll of Honour are

Lance-Corporal H. Leah. Lance-Corporal Spruce. Private G. Green (Earlestown). Private W. H. Hutson. Private G. Edwards. Private J. Smith. Private R. H. (or W.) Hill. Private R. Marshall. Private A. E. Freeman. Private A. J. Woods. Private J. Blain. Private J. Williams (Golborne). Private R. Hamblett. Private A. Roberts. Private E. Jackson. Private J. Liptrott. Private J. Litherland. Private Charles Frederick Malkin, son of the late Councillor Malkin, who lived with his Other at 6, Elmw,ood-avenue, has been killed

without recovering consciousness. On be- half of myself and my comrades I offer you and yours, in this, the hour of your trouble, our deepest sympathy. ===== 0002601_19150605_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
DIED OF WOUNDS.

PRIVATE F. S. PERCIVAL.

Lingered Nine Days With Bullet

Wound In Head.

Private Frederick Samuel Percival, 81, Priestley-street, who was wounded by a bullet in the head on May 9th, as reported in "The Examiner" May 15th, died of. his wounds on May 18th.

His father is employed in Messrs. Crosfield's glycerine department. Birthday in the Firing-Line.

Writing home on April 25th, the Private remarked :

"I shall have my eighteenth birthday in the firing-line! The trenches where we were are very comfortable to what they have been. Where we had to stand knee-deep in mud and water a few weeks back is now quite dry; the weather is fine. We have paid the Huns a bit of what we owe them. We caught a working party in front of their trenches, and I give you my word we scattered a few before they got back to the safety of their own trench."

WOUNDED.

LANCE-CORPORAL 11. E. HOLMES.

Shot Whilst Fixing Barbed-Wire Entanglements.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
Mother Must Feel Proud Of Him.

The distressing intelligence was conveyed in the following sympathetic letter, signed "Katherine, Sister-in-charge" of the hospi- tal at which the gallant "Terrier" breathed his last, and written to a gentleman in War- rington who is acquainted with the family : "Will you kindly inform Mrs. Percival, 81, Priestley-street, Warrington, of the death of her son-2210 • Private Percival, 4th South Lancashire 'Regiment? He was brought in here (2nd Clearing Station, General Head- quarters, British Expeditionary Force) 11th May, 1915, very severely wounded in the head and quite unconscious. Everything possible has been done for him by the surgeons and nurses, but, in spite of all we did, he never regained consciousness. Mrs. Percival will, I know, feel the death of her son very much, but she must feel proud of him for having given his life so bravely for the honour of our country, and she must feel relieved to know that after being wounded he received every care and attention. Please -convey to his parents our sincere sympathy. I think if you will kindly break the sad news to his mother that she may be spared much pain." The letter was dated May 18th.

"For goodness sake don't tell them anything about me." This is a quotation from a letter written by Lance-Corporal Horace Edward Holmes, of the Warrington Territorials, to his mother, Mrs. A. Holmes, of 15, Scott-street, Warrington. Lance-Corporal Holmes has been wounded in the right leg, and, writing home to say how it happened, gives ,expression to the above, exhortation, "them" being. a reference to the local Pressmen. At the risk of incurring the wrath of this modest hero, we are putting him into print.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
"Quite d Lad."

On May 17th the Matron of the hospital wrote to Mrs. P. Mortimer, of Crofton Lodge, Kingsley, near Frodsham, as follows "Mrs. Percival's son quite a lad) has been brought into this hospital, No. 2 Casualty Clearing Station, very severely wounded in his head and unconscious, and we are doing all we can; and to-day he is rather more sensible and has taken some food; but I very much fear he will never really recover. I found a letter in his pocket from his mother, and she was begging him to write, as she would be anxious."

LANCE-CORPORAL HOLMES. Wounded.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
Fell Into the Barbed Wire.

"We were putting wire entanglements out at one o'clock on Sunday night (Monday morning), when I was shot," he writes. "You would have laughed to see me jump when I got hit, and then fall amongst the barbed wire. It took five minutes to get me out when they came back. I had sent them all back for some more wire, so I had to wait fur nearly an hour before I was found. . . . They are getting beaten back now, so I suppose our chaps will have a rest, and they deserve it, after fifteen days in the trenches. .

===== 0002601_19150605_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
A Good Marksman.

Lance-Corporal Holmes joined the Territorials two years or more ago. He was one of the first to volunteer for the Front, and "signed on" on his twenty-first birthday. He is the holder of the D Company Recruits' Cup for shooting and general efficiency. Prior to his enlistment in August he was employed at the Vulcan Foundry, Earlestown. He was educated at Hamilton-street School. His brother Alfred. who is in the sth Lancers, was wounded at Mons on the 23rd August, and is now a shoesmith instructor at Dublin. Another brother, Frank, is in the Territorials. and he, too, has been wounded, meeting with an injury on the 4th March. He is still in hospital at Rotten.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE ERNEST JACKSON.

"One Of The Cheeriest Chaps In The Section" flit By Splinter.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
"A Slight Accideno

A letter was received by her from Corporal J. Fleming, of "D" Company, which ran as follows :

"Just a few lines, in which your husband wishes me' to inform you of a slight accident with which he met in the trenches this morn- ing (May 20th). He was sitting in his dug- out, along with two other chaps, when a rifle- grenade exploded about three yards from their door, and all three got hit by splinters from it. One got hit in the hand, while your hus- band and the other chap got hit in -the top part of the leg. Luckily, it was not serious, and I assure . you there is nothing to .worry about. I saw the wound myself and dressed it. . . . We will miss him very much during the short time he is away from us, for he is one of the cheeriest chaps in the section,. and it is a pleasure to work with him."

Injured Man Writes That He Is "Doing Fine.','

Later, Mrs. Jackson received a letter from her husband, evidently written from a base hospital, in which he said he was doing fine, and expressed the hope that the news the* he had been wounded did not give his wife a ehock, the same as the Germans gave him.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
Footballer and Friendly Society Man.

Private Jackson, who is 24 years of age, is a member of "D" Company. His time as a Territorial had expired, for he had • servcd four ye,ats, liut, upon the outbreak of war, he re-joined. He formerly worked in the galvan- ising department at Messrs. Itylands Bios., Ltd., and he played football for his Oepart- ment. As a boy he attended the Wycliffe and the National Schools. He is a member of the Oddfellows. Private Jackson's cousin is a prisoner-of-war in Germany.

PRIVATE R. 11. HILL

===== 0002601_19150605_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
Hoped Comrades Shared Ills Parcels

Of Good Things.

Private Richard Henry Hill, known to his many friends as "Dick." received a bullet- wound in the right thigh on May 13th. He is 25 years of age and he joined the "Terriers" after the outbreak of .war, viz., on August 9th. When in Warrington he made his home with his sister, at 15, Vere-street. He is the son of Mr. Walter Hill, of 18, Chorley-street, who himself served more than a quarter of a cen- tury in the Citizen Army, viz., the old Volun- teers, of whom the "Terriers" are, so to speak, lineal descendants. Private R. H. Hill is a native of Warring- ton, though for some years he lived at Abram, near Wigan, and only returned to the town of his birth about twelve months ago. Prior to offering himself for active service he was employed by Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. at their St. Helens Brewery, travelling backwards and forwards daily to his work. At present Private Hill is in the 16th

PRIVATE R. H. HILL. Wounded.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
Genetral Hospital, France, and the latest news to hand says that he is making good progress towards recovery. "Chanced To Stop A German Bullet."

Writing to his sister on the 26th of last month, he said: "I am out of bed again and am walking about. . . . I would like some- body to send me a box of pigs!. . . . I did not get the parcels you sent, because I hap- pened to come out of ' the trench just about the time they were due, and then I chanced to stop a German bullet with my thigh! . . . You have no need to get worried about my wound—it is getting on very well. It was a good job the bullet went right through, for if it had stopped in it would have made mat- ters worse. , I expect to be shifted, from here in four or five days. . . . . . and to be going to another hospital in France for a rest, when I hope to be able to write to my mates," Praise for the Field Hospitals.

In an earlier letter he announced the fact that he had been wounded and said that it was a good job that there were such well- appointed field hospitals to look after the men who are hit. "I could not," he says, "have believed there were such places; they are splendid, and what they do for you is past praise. . . . We have plenty of food of all kinds. . . Poor Tommy Hayes, who was killed, was in the same trench as I was. . .

Keep your heart up; I am quite happy here." In another letter he Said that his leg was causing him",bonsiderable pain,, and, added that in addition to getting plenty of good food he was waited on band and food; "indeed," he wrote, "it is marvellous what they do for you." He added that if his sister had for- warded any parcels to the trenches for him since he was wounded he hoped the lads would open them up and divide them amongst them when they got back to their billets." Brother Fought In South Africa.

Private Hill's elder brother, Walter, was formerly a lance-corporal in the old Volun- teers, with which he served in the South African War, and holds a medal with five bars. Private Hill's father formed one of the guard of honour at Queen Victoria's visit to Liverpool in 1886. Private Hill was educated at the Silver- street Schools and he is a member of the Sons of Temperance.

PRIVATE J. LITHERLAND.

A Nasty Flesh-Wound In The

Private John Litherland has been wounded in the leg, and is at present in hospital in Bir- mingham. He is the son of Mr. Wm. Lither- land, of 9, Winwick-road, Warrington. News that he had been hit was first received in a letter written by a comrade and intimate friend, Private Charles Ryan, and counter-signed by another friend, Private John Coyne. "Not in a Bad Way." The letter ran :

"Just a line or two to inform you that your son; Jack; has been wounded. I may tell you that he is not in a bad way—he has got a flesh wound in the calf of his leg. He has

asked me to write to you. Jack Coyne, an- other of his pals, is writing to some of your Jack's many friends in Tunbridge Wells. He will be coming home to you before many days are over. I do envy him. He got his wound op the very job I should have been doing, but I went to a village not very far away to church. Jack had not this opportunity, be- cause there are .no churches of his religion in Belgium, so he said he would do the job for me. After his doing this much for me, you may be surprised at my saying that I envy

===== 0002601_19150605_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
The grenade which killed Privates John Hayes and Frank Stokes, has, it appears, been a 159 responsible for the wounding of Private Albert James Wood, who, prior to "joining up," lived with his wife at 21, Vere-street, and who now lies in Salford Royal Hospital. He wrote the following letter to his wife soon after he was wounded :—" Just a line to let you know of my ill-luck; I am sorry to tell you that I have been wounded. It is nothing to fear; it is in my foot and calf." Private Wood is now progressing very nicely. He joined the battalion on the outbreak of war. He was employed by Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd., .and is a native of Warrington. He was educated at St. Mary's School, Buttermarket-street, and attended St. Benedict's Church.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE GEORGE EDWARDS.

The news that Private George Edwards, of the Warrington Territorials, had been wounded was first conveyed to his mother, Mrs. William- soh, of ,Priestley-street, Warrington, who has been twice married, in a letter written by a

Lance-Corporal Edward Spruce, of 449, Well- field-street, Warrington, was doing duty with the " Terriers" on May 22nd, when he was wounded in the foot owing to the bursting of a shrapnel shell. The gallant Lance-Corporal is the son of Mr. Harry Spruce, and he was educated at St. Bar- nabas' School. Twenty-two years of age, he is a single man, and, prior to going to the Front, made his home with his father. He was serving with C Company when he met with his injury. He only joined the Territorials at the outbreak of war, and, having trained at Tunbridge Wells, he went to the Front in September.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
"Going on Very Nicely."

This was the second time he had been wounded, for, a little over a month before he got the present hit in the foot, he was injured, but, fortunately, the hurt was only a slight one. and he soon recovered from it, and resumed duty. _ _ _

A-letter received from him on May 28th inti- mated that he had been Moved to another hos- pital than the one in which he was first quar- tered—one further down the line. "I am," he adds, "going on very nicely, but am still un- able to walk, save with the aid of my crutches. The hospital in which I am quartered is a fine place. We get well looked after, and we are having fine weather. . . . Just before I got wounded we had a good dose of gas, and it was terrible. I think we are going to use the stuff . . . At the time I was wounded sixty shells had been fired into our trenches. Terrible things they are! . . . I was lying on a stretcher three days before they .brought me away from the dressing station. Ido not think that the war will last long, now that Italy has started."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
A Chapel Chorister and a Rechabite.

Lance-Corporal Spruce has a good voice, and prior to the war was a member of the choir of the Legh-street Primitive Methodist Chapel. He is a member of the Order of RechaNtes, and takes a deep interest in football, at,. one time Playing with the St. Barnabas' team in the Warrington au4 District Associatiun League.

PRIVATE JOSEPH SMITH.

Hit By A Stray Btillet.

Private Joseph Smith, who has been slightly wounded, is a married man, and lives with his wife at 18, Elizabeth-street. Mrs. Smith's brother is a stretcher-hearer with the Terri- torials, and he wrote to her concerning her hus- band as follows :

"I am sorry to tell you that Joe was wotrnded by a stray bullet, which hit him in the thigh. There is no cause to worry, as the wound is not serious. Joe %'as hit while changing over from another trench to the firing trench. . . . He

was cheerful gill the way to the dressing' station, having very little pain." Private Smith, who is 29 years of age, was educated at Wycliffe School. He was. employed as a wheel-maker at the Dallam Forge, and later as a coal trimmer at Croaeld's Works. He joined the Territorials at the outbreak of the war.

PRIVATE W. 11. HUTSON.

Private W. H. Hutson, of 7, Jockey-street, Warrington, has been wounded. The first in- timation of his injury was conveyed to his wife and family of four in a letter from Private J. E. Watson, who made the reassur- ing statement that the wound was not serious, and added the cheerful information that Private Hutson would be in. England in a few weeks' time. This letter was dated May 11th.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
Brother's Gift Turned the Bullet.

The day after receiving the first letter, Ms. Hutson had one from her husband, stating that he was getting on favourably and would soon be home. , "You can tell Walter," the letter went on, "that the ' fag ' lighter he sent me saved me from being ruined for life, as it turned the bullet."

Following In Father's Footsteps.

Vacating his situation at Messrs. Richmond's Grappenhall works on October Ist, Private Hutson joined the 4th South Lancashire Regi- ment. As a boy he attended St. Benedict's School. His father is an old soldier and served 29 years in the Northumberland Fusiliers.

Private Hutson is now at home. He came from the Ist Northern General Hospital at Newcastle 'on Wednesday.

I_,Y MM

Another Batch of Beigians.—At "Beechwood," Lymni, arrived on Thursday another batch of Belgian refugees. There they are now quite happily and comfortably installed.

Council and Late Lieutenant Ridgway.-31r. Gotobed's tribute to the late Lieut. Althorp Ridgway—whose death from wounds in the Dardanelles land fighting we reported last week —appears on another page. The Motor Accident.—Mr. Marsh, Bradley Hall, Cherry-lane, Lymm, informs us that he was not near the motor accident, which we re- ported last week, and in which Mr. Edward Gil- berts was knocked down.

Retorts Supply.—The Urban Council have approved the acceptance of the tender of Messrs. Drakes, Limited, of Halifax, for the supply and fixing of ,retorts—recommended from a meeting of the Gas Committee held on May 25th. Sir A. H. Crosfield, Bart., and Lymm.—The honour conferred this week upon Sir Arthur H. Crosfield, Bart., is also no small honour to the Lymm township—as in it the baronet spent his boyhood, residing with his parents at "Bay- cliff," at present the residence of Mr. Boum- phrey.

Boys and Street Lamps.--Referring to the recent breaking of street lamps by boys, the Chairman of the Urban Ccancii. (Councillor Gotdbed) told an offender at Monday night's meeting that any boys breaking glass in the streets that belongs to the ratepayers will be brought before the magistrates.—Coun(illor Cowper (chairman of the Gas Committee) em- phasised the fact.

A Touching Thought.—A. Lymm soldier writes home to his old parents: "I was just thinking, before Writing, this, about the times when I was a little boy athome. This would have been the day when I should have had my new suit on; and here I am out here •ding my duty, and I am proud to do it with a4,,00d heart, and go to the trenches just as if I was going to meet my young lady."

Sergeant E. James Promoted.—News has been received that Sergt. E. James, son of Mr. C. James, of Statham Brickworks, has received a further promotion. Since going out to France early in February as a private, his promotion has been rapid. After a few weeks he was made corporal, which was quickly followed by his rising to the rank of sergeant. He is now com- pany-sergeant-major, attached to 'C 'Company .of the sth -Cheshire Territorials.

Will Partington Use Fire Engine?—At a meeting of the Lymm Building and Fire Engine Committee held on May 25th it was decided that, in addition to the fixed scale of charges laid down by the Council in cases of fires, an annual retaining fee of £7 10s. be asked from the Partington Parish -Council— who had written asking for terms for the use of the engine and brigade.—The Council have confirmed the minute.

Youthful Pugilists.—Yesterday •about mid- day quite a crowd collected on Booth's Hill to witness a startling contest between two youth- ful enemies of decided pugilistic tendencies. A few, minutes afterwards the battle—of which the fury by now quite rivalled that of the greater ones abroad—was abruptly concluded, the antagonists beating an undignified retreat each with an ear between the fingers of his worthy mother. Stiles On Field Footpaths.—At a ,meeting of the Lymm Roads Committee held on May 25th it was resolved: "That letters be written to the following requesting them to repair stiles on field footpaths:—Misses Woodall for foot- path near Massey Brook, and Mr. Heald fer footpath near Isolation Hospital; also that a letter be written to the Misses. Woodall calling attention to the condition of the retaining wall near Parr's Bank, Underbridge."—This decision has been approved by the Council.

In Attendance.—At a meeting of file Lymm Urban District Council held at "Whitharrow" —the Council offices—on Monday evening, there were present: Councillors R. H. Gotobed (in the chair), C. E. Cowper, S. Critchley, W. G. Groves, A. Maclntyre and G. 11. Parekr; to- gether with Messrs. W. Mullard (clerk), W. Timperley and Cecil Mort (assistant clerks), D. Mort (surveyor), T. H. West (gas manager), and C. I. Barnes (water manager); and Dr. E. M. De Jong (medical officer of health).

What a Lady Did.—Some young fellows would have fairly blushed with shame on Monday afternoon, could they have witnessed the futile, but nevertheless noble and patriotic, endeavour Of a Lymm lady to enlist. With grim deter- mination she visited the local recruiting officer, whom she proceeded to inform that her one desire was to don the khaki; and it was only after a long argument that that worthy gentle- man succeeded in convincing his fair visitor of the futility of her desire. One cannot but think that had the lady's wish been granted she would have made a good soldier.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
LIELT. ALTHORP RIDG WAY.

Lymm Council Chairman's Tribute.

Before the Lymm .Urban District Councillors commenced business at their monthly meeting on Monday evening, The Chairman (Mr. R. IL Gotobed) said: Since we last met, a family which has been, and still is, closely associated..with this Coun- cil, has suffered a heavy bereavement. I refer to the death of Lieutenant Althorp Ridgway— from wounds received in recent fighting in the defence of his country. We shall all desire to express our deep sympathy and con- dolence with Mr. and Mrs. Ridgway and family in the loss of a young life of such great pro- mise. Though thousands of miles away he heard his country's call and gallantly gave his life—a shining example to thousands of our young men at home, who as yet have not real- ised their duty. I move that the 'Clerk be instructed to convey to Mr. and Mrs. Ridgway our deep sympathy. Mr. W. G. Groves seconded, as one who had known the family and had been associated with the Council during a part of the time that Mr. Ridgway, son., was connected with it.

The motion was assented to in silence.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE'S SYMPATHY.

Mr. T. J. Ridgway presided on Tuesday afternoon over a meeting of the Lymm Ad- ministrative Sub-Committee, and after having retired from the room, Mr. S. W. Groves (deputy-chairman) took the , chair, and referred feelingly and in very sympathetic terms to the sad death of Lieut. J. Althorp Ridgway, their chairman's second son—who had died from wounds received in action. Mr. Groves moved:— " That this meeting place on record, and offer to Mr. and Mrs. Ridgway and family, their deep sympathy and condolence in the great and heavy loss sustained by them; and that the Clerk be directed to convey to Mr. and Mrs. Ridgway a copy of this resolution?' This was carried in silence. At Tuesday night's meeting of the War- rington Town Council sympathetic reference was also made to the death of the young Lieutenant.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR WORK FOR WOMEN.

How The Fair Sex Can Help To Beal

===== 0002601_19150605_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
The Germans.

GOVERNMENTS SCHEME EXPLAINED.

At the Drill Hall on Wednesday night a large audience of ladies listened to a lucid exposi- tion of the Government's scheme for the .em- ployment of women in the .present national emergency, in order that more men may be re- leased for service in the fighting forces. The Hon. J. E. Cross was in the chair, and he was supported on the platform by Vice-Chancellor Stewart Smith, MT. W. Peter Rylands., Mr. A. Wafkin, and other prominent local residents.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
The Hint of Compulsory Service.

In the course of a few introductory remarks, the Chairman emphasised the need for women volunteering to fill the places of. some of the men who had gone to the Front, and added that the need was doubly important in view of the fact that the late Government had already hinted that there might be some sort of compul- sory national service. If that were brought about it would take away many young men who were at present working in that immediate dis- trict, as well, of course, as in others. There might not be .many opportunities for women to serve in Lymm itself, and yet the place was closely allied with one very great interest which affected the whole of the country, and that was the farming industry—an industry which they could not do without, and one in which women could render valuable assistance; indeed, in many countries, and, in fact, in many counties in this very country, they did do so. In Here- fordshire, in the North of England, and in Scot- land, it was quite a common thing to find the womenfolk working on the farms, and what the women in those parts of the country could do. surely the women in the neighbourhood of Lymm could do also. They. too, could put their hand to the plough, and, by so doing, release some young men for the Front.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
Women to Whom the Appeal is Addressed.

The meeting was then addressed at length by Miss Knowles, the Senior Organising Officer for Women's Work, under the Board of Trade Labour Exchanges, who explained that the object of the scheme for women's work at the present moment was to get a census, so that the Government might be in a position, should the necessity arise, of knowing how many more men could be released for active service without injury to the vital industries of the country, and to what extent the places of such men could be filled ,by women. There were two classes of women to whom the appeal, was addresed—the leisured class, who were not obliged to work, but who might be willing to take paid employ- ment from patriotic reasons, should necessity arise, and the men have to go to the Front; find the retired class—women wlio rhad worked at some time in their lives, but who did not now work, the necessity for earning their livings having, ceased, owing to marriage or some other cause. The appeal was not addresseil to the ordinflry unemployed; the Labour Exchanges would" deal'with them.' The speaker went on to indicate ways in which women could be of great service, and emphasised the fact that the work they undertook must be paid work; it must not, under any circumstances, be voluntary work.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
The Women Volunteers Must Be Paid.

If women came forward during the war period and gays their services .gratis, they would be placing the men at the Front at tremendous disadvantage, for they would be producing the evil of cheap labour, and thus making it hard for the men to get back into their jobs when they returned from the war. No, the work the women did must be paid, and paid for at proper rates. (Hear, hear.) The women who now came forward must be paid equal wages for equal value of work done. Another important point *was that the work taken up by women now must be taken up for the duration of the war only; the women who came forward must be prepared to retire in favour of the men when they came home and wanted their old jobs back. (Hear, hear.) That must be an absolutely understood condition. "This war work for women," said the speaker, by way of conclu- sion, "represents the call of the country to you, the women of rLymm—of England. It is the chance the women have been waiting for since the War started, isn't it? (Applause.) We are not going to be behind the men in our loyalty; we are not going to be behind the women of France, are we?" (Renewed applause.)

===== 0002601_19150605_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
If Germany Won -

Mr. W. Peter Itylands next addressed the meeting, and said it had been uttered as a re- proach that the people of this country did not seem to realise that they were at war, and he could not quite make up his mind whether that was true or not. Perhaps the explanation was that, as a nation, they were rather phlegmatic; they were always very confident, and had a quiet way of going on, a way which did not reveal itself in -a demonstrative form, even when they were feeling deeply, or suffering pain. The apathy, which they had heard spoken about, was, he believed, more apparent than real; it was confined to a few who had very small in- terests in the- war itself, and it did not come home to them. (Hear, hear.) That there was need for the country to exert itself to the full would be readily seen if they, for a bare moment, contemplafed the consequences of de- feat; nay, those consequences were too terrible even to contemplate. The sacrifices they were asked to make to-day were as nothing compared with those that they would be compelled to make if Britain were beaten and humiliated. (Hear, hear.) Everyone of them ought always to be thinking of the war; thinking to them- selves, "Is there anything which I can do to assist my country at this juncture."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
Every Young Man's Duty.

Every man of military age must be doing something to help his country, and not merely doing something to help himself. (Hear, hear.) If he was not engaged in making munitions or making other things that were absolutely essen- tial to the successful carrying on of the war, he must go to the Front and fight. "That," concluded Mr. Rylands, "is what I believe is in front of us, and the sooner it comes the better I believe it will be for our nation." (Applause.) On the proposition of Vice-Chancellor Smith, seconded by Mr. A. Parmeter, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the chairman and speakers

===== 0002601_19150605_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE VOLUNTEERS.

Recruiting Parades Much In Demand.

The Lymm detachment of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment is splendidly progressing. It now has fifty members—who, in their new uniforms, look very smart indeed. As regards efficiency they have been Making rapid strides, and can boast no less-than eleven maiksmen— the highest point to be attained in firing. At Heatley on Tuesday evening the Lymm detachment held a recruiting parade. Accom- panied by the Heatley Boy Scouts' Bugle Band they marched to the parade ground, and there Captain G. Williamson delivered a rousing speech to the large crowd which assembled. The effort has, we understand, proved very successful.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
To-day at Altrincham.

This afternoon at Market Square, Altrine- ham, 'the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment will parade to aid recruiting for their ranks. The Lymm platoon will attend—commanded by Captain George Williamson, and Sergeants J. Lister-Farrar and S. Moston. The band for the parade will be the regimental hand—lately the Altrinciaam Borough Band.

Lymm SOll-11 AFRICAN

===== 0002601_19150605_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
LYIVIIVI TERR ITORIALS.

ESTI% 'MID LOVELV SCENERY

Sergeant Harold Clare Ivrifing ur date May '2sth to Mr. F. E. INttaws, tult of The Cross, 6.113"8 _ _ .

'4I think we have been 'very 'lucky indeed; and I am glad to give you the good news that we have left the trenches for a time, and are now enjoying a wellreurned rest which will do everybody good. ,

"We are billeted at a farm on a hill, and we have a beautiful view of a pretty country. It is a great treat to be away from Itte firing-line—arra, I may say, the bad smells of the trenches. For there are a tremendous amount of dead bodies there.

"The health of our boys is geed, but suCh a lot are 111—but not serious. Our 'boys 'do stick, don't they And I know how knocked up they have felt. They have done splendid and they deserve the rest. . . Glad to report no casualties."

Private Richard Shaw has now practically recovered from the wound in his leg, and on Thursday evening journeyed to another town in the county, to rejoin a section of his regi- ment. Thus he is with Lance-Corporals Linley and Blease, and Private Arthur Hadden—all of whose cases have resembled his.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE. GATES OF HELL."

ance-Corporal Reg. Hillier Tells Of

His Wounding.

From Lance-Corporal Reginald Hillier, son of Mr. W. E. Hillier, outfitter, Lymm—the news of whose being wounded whilst serving in the,Dar- danelles with the 1/6th Manchester Territorials we gave last week--comes now the following letter, dated May 19th, and headed St. Andrew's Barracks Hospital, Malta :- "You will see where I am .now. It is a fine

hospital on a hill, at the back of the town, everlooking the sea. We arrived here last night,

having been on the water since last Sunday week [May 9th]. I am expecting to have the bullet taken out of my leg to-day; it has worked itself to the surface. I shall be all right again in a few weeks' time.

"Ramsey [his friend] and I were both wounded about the same time—about eight o'clock on the Saturday night. We were both dressed together at the base, and put in the same tent to rest before being taken on board ship; but he was taken away before me, as his wound was rather severe—in the head, and also one in the shoul- der;. so I don't know where he is now.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
Day and Night Bombardment.

"Well, I have had the experience of a life time. In plain English, the gates of hell were opened. All day long, and through the night, the bombarding was going on. From the sea the battleships were 'going on '—including the Queen Elizabeth; then the English and French artillery; then the machine guns and rifle fire; and last, but not least, the shrapnel. And, of course, the enemy was letting us have it as well.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
Carrying Shells to the Guns.

"On the Friday (the day before I was wounded) a terrible bombardment started; the French and English artillery were doing it. We were in the rest trenches, just between the Eng- lish and French batteries, and the noise was horrible. Just think of it! Every minute a shell; here we were, all peeping over the trenches to see the shells bursting. The Turk- ish shells (shrapnel) were whistling over our heads, but none burst over us; they had got a bad range. You should have seen us running along a gully, carrying shells to our guns. That wis the only ad quickest way to get them there, and we did it. We got orders to move up again this night, and hold a ridge—which we did, and were still holding on the Saturday night when I stopped this shot. So I have no idea how the battalion are going on now; but they will be doing their dutv, I know.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0199.txt | included=true | included =====
Near a "75."

"If you come across any soldier who has been in France ask him What the sound of a French 75' is like. Well, one of these was firing a hundred yards from my right. Yes, I can still hear it. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
"ANOTHER BIG BATTLE."

Corporal Ben Elliott Says "We Are Advancing All The Time."

Writing from France just before his promo- tion, Corporal Ben Elliott, of the Scots Guards, told his sister, Miss Millie Elliott, at Altrincham : "I am in the best of health. We have just arrived from the trenches, and have got into a small village after being in another big battle. We are advancing all the time now, so we are constantly on the .move. So if you don't hear from me don't be alarmed, be- cause I can't write while we are always mov- ing. We have only come out here to get some more men

===== 0002601_19150605_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
"Kaiser's Fancy Men."

"We charged the Germans on Sunday morn- ing at 3.15—1 guess you were peacefully sleep- ing then. However, we were up against some of the Kaiser's fancy men, and I can tell you we made them run, too! "But it was awful to see the dead and wounded' after we had finished with them. I put my bayonet and gun into a German, and I left it there—stuck in him—and got another gun, and went on. - And before nine o'clock in the morning we had, taken three lines of trenches from them. And then they tried to get back; but didn't our boys make them sorry they ever tried! "I got a lot more souvenirs out of the Ger- man trenches—a revolver and field glasses; but I'm afraid I can't carry them about.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
"We Simply Went Into It."

"Our Commanding Officer told us to-day he was proud of us all, as we were the only regiment that reached the German trenches. Everybody seemed as if he didn't ,care; we simply went into it amidst very heavy fire. However, I thank God I have come through it, and I am ready to go into another battle when our time comes again. "Well, we are just simply covered with mud; you would think we were a lot of navvies. But I don't mind as long as we are giving it tc), them for the tricks they have done on oui ships. We will pay them back; the Scots Guards are the men that can do it for them!" -

===== 0002601_19150605_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
"Our Minister."

To his parents the Corporal wrote on May 23rd: "I hive just returned from church. Of course, our church out here is a big field, but I must say I have enjoyed the service very much. Last Sunday we were in a big battle; we did very well, and made a name for our battalion. I can tell you we made the Ger- mans run! Every man was anxious to get at them, and we went over their trenches like going in a race. . . . I wouldn't mind if we were in the German trenches again to-day. . .

"We took those German trenches at Festu- bert—you will have seen the name in the paper. They had tried four times before to take the same trenches, hut had failed.

"We have no fear whatever—even with shells bursting all around us we keep on smiling. Of course, .we get our minister in the trenches to 'see us, and he gives us all courage. He was right up ,in the German trenches with us, having a, chat and passing a joke now and again; and it made us All very keen on our duty. • . ."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
Inspected By General Joffre.

The Corporal's latest word to his sister runs: "I am pleased to inform you of my promotion to Corporal. . . I took two stripes on 27th May, and in the afternoon we were inspected by• the French General—General .loffre. And as we have the honour of being the first lot of British troops he has ever inspected it was quite a busy day. . .

" BEECIIWOOD " GAS.

Councillor . Co‘rper's Allegations Against Belgians.

COLLEAGUES' DISSENT.

At the monthly meeting of the Lymm Urban District Council, held on 'Monday evening! and presided over by Councillor It. H. Gotobed, Councillor C. E. Cowper said: As chairman of the Gas Committee I should like to point out that we are not in great funds, and are supplying the "Beechwood " Belgians with gas free; and yet they have some hundreds of pounds in hand, besides getting money from Sir William Lever. We see the men there drinking, and no doubt enjoying themselves. They are not poor people, and some of them are able to fight for their country as well as my sons. I think, under the circumstances, the District Council: should not be .called upon to supply gas free. If it was for a convalescent home of our soldiers we would de everything we could; we would not stint them, but would do everything on earth to assist them. But I travel "a lot; and wherever I go—north, south, east; west—l find there is a great deal of discontent about the [Belgian] people; and, considering all things, they Should be made to pay for the gas they use. The Chairman: You refer to the committee; not the refugees? Councillor Cowper: Yes; .and I say that in future I strongly urge you to let them pay. I could mention names. One man's of age—a gentleman whom we all like. I asked him how ' old he was, and he said "Thirty-one." I think the committee has a lot of money in hand, and in future I strongly urge you gentlemen to make them pay—as they can! I move that the gas in future be charged for.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLEGATION OF DRINKING.

There was no seconder. Councillor Groves: I shall not second it! I did not like one remark Mr. Cowper made, and I don't think he ought to have made it; it ought not to go forth from this Council. I refer to the remark about drinking. I think that we ought not to let it go forth that he said anything about drinking. Councillor Cowper: I should very much like to onen my mouth to a worse state, but I won't do it. I can open my mouth!

===== 0002601_19150605_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
"ONLY AT FIRST."

The Chairman: When the refugees first came I believe there were cases; but those refugees have been gone for some considerable time, and I don't think there is anything of that kind now at all. Personally, I think if you had limited your remarks to the committee paying for the gas you would get more support. But one doesn't want to associate himself with those remarks. Those young men who are un- wounded certainly should be doing their share; but, apart from. that, there are a good number of very respectable women, and I think you will get more support for your resolution if you will confine yourself to those remarks. Councillor Cowper: Mr. Chairman, I travel a good deal, and I see it everywhere that they are drinking and won't work! The Chairman: There is no seconder, Mr. Cowper, Councillor Cowper: No? Well, what I say can go forth.

STOCKTON HEATH.

"FED LP MTH THE PLACE.

Grocer Tells Of Ills Failure ha

Bankruptcy Court.

At the Warrington Bankruptcy Court yester- day morning, Franklin Bennett, who had car- ried on the business of a grocer, &c., in Stock- ton Heath, appeared for his public examination, his statement of affairs disclosing liabilities esti- mated at £449 and a deficiency of over £3OO. In answer to the Official Receiver the debtor detailed his business ventures since he started in partnership with his brother at Moss Side, Manchester, in 1885, and pointed out that up to the time he came to Stockton Heath in 1908 he had traded successfully. His coming to Stock- ton Heath, he said, had proved a bad specula- tion right through, and he "bad had to fight like grim death against the Co-op." At first it was necessary to cut prices in order to get a footing, and then, as soon as he had put prices up to a level that would yield something like a fair percentage of profit, his customers dropped away and "went to the Co-op. for the sake of the almighty dividend !" Unless the people, he said, went to the Stores for their goods many of, the neighbours practically boycotted them, and had he known that the circumstances were any- thing like what they proved to be he would never have set foot in the district; it had proved his undoing. He had encountered no such dan- gerous rivals during his experience as a shop- keeper in the suburbs of Manchester, "where they found enlightened .people." The representative of a creditor asked the debtor, "Were you 'fed up' with the shop and everything about Stockton Heath?" "I should think I am now !" was the debtor's answer. "And so you set about to deliberately kill the business, didn't you?" pursued the questioner. "Not likely !" the debtor answered. The examinatiou was closed.
