VICAR AND COIIPULSION.

Bound To Come, Says Rev. W. Bracecamp.

In a letter in St. Paul's Parish Magazine for June, the Rev. W. Bracecamp, says : "The cry is still for more • men, and I do hope all the eligible young men in Warrington will listen to the call before they are compelled to come. Compulsion, in my humble opinion, is bound to come, therefore, I hope every mother will encourage her boy 'to join before that .comes. It _is a perfect disgrace to us as a nation to know that so many are holding back. The Germans must be made to suffer for their outrageous conduct to our men. It is impos- sible to realise that a nation which has boasted of her culture should do such low, down acts. Savages would hesitate to do what has already been done by our enemy. We must see this thing through, to the very end, no matter what it costs in sacrifice. If the Germans do land here they w,ill spare nothing, and they will repeat their devilish acts upon our own kith and kin."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
" ELMWOOD " ANNIVERSARY.

Striking Discourses And Fine Music

—X Great Success.

Life has a meaning, and Jesus Christ said you can spell it out in terms of God, who knew about every sparrow that fell to the ground. You may not believe it, but He said it! You think it out—that in these days when we talk about wiping out regiments, and when we get casualty lists yards long, God knows about every sparrow that falls to the ground! And I am inclined that Jesus Christ knew more about it than we do.

. So spoke the Rev. T. T. James, Pastor of Wycliffe, in one of the striking discourses which he on Sunday delivered at the Elmwood- avenue Congregational Church. The occa- sion was the anniversary celebration of the Sunday School—marked throughout by com- plete success., Upon the Minister (the Rev. C. P. Pitt). the Choirmaster (Mr. W. H. Wal- lington), and all the happy band of workers, the whole reflected very great credit. .

===== 0002601_19150605_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
GOD'S OPTIMISM.

Basing his morning remarks upon the words from Romans: "Now the God of hope fill you with all joy and peace; in believing ye may abound in hope," Mr. James (who conducted the services) said that sometimes it was one word we needed about God and sometimes it was another. Of course we summed up the whole meaning of God by saying that He was Love; but there were many different ways in which we might think of the relationship of God to ourselves. This text just presented God as the great central fount of hopefulness. "I don't know," continued the preacher, "that there is anything we need more to talk about to-day than abounding in hope. There is plenty in the world outside to take away hopefulness and gladness, in the noise and tumult of battle and in the tremendous sacri- fice of human life. Then perhaps many come here this morning fully aware of the failure of their characters. We need to-day the spirit of buoyant optimism. And when we realise that God is full of optimism we can get it into our own hearts; for we discover God's optimism in the whole history of . the universe, and in the great storyilbf evolution. Like the scientist goes over and over again the same experiment in the great hope that one day he is going to realise something, noth- ing keeping him back, so we see God working away at the world in its evolution and growth age after age. And you are only going to explain it by saying that in His heart there is a great living hope that some day His pur- pose and His plan are to be finished and per- fected. The tragedy- of the Cross did not take place just in order that one day the world might run more smoothly, but because every soul was eternal In the sight of God, and had a destiny." Faith in these truths and that buoyant optimism were the surest, best and only foundation for the spirit that could ever look forward knowing that all was well.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
"UNDER THE PALMS."

For the afternoon service the building was completely filled. Mr. Pitt presided and the choir and scholars gave a splendid and in- spiring rendering of the well-known sacred cantata "Under the Palms." The finely- balanced and harmonised choruses proved most effective, and each soloist gave a particu- larly pleasing interpretation of his or her part. The individual singers were: Mrs. F. Longshaw; the Misses Doris Travis, Ethel Wilson, Lucy Pearson, Florence Hunt, Ethel Wells and Effie Leathley; Mrs. Hazeldine, aml Messrs. J. W. Travis, Hazeldine, Crabtree and Mather. Mr. W. H. Wallington conducted (at all the services), Mr. Reg. Wallington officiated at the organ, and Miss A. Leathley at the piano.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LORDSHIP OF CHRIST.

Mr. James took his ,evening tekt from. Philippians, and preached to a crowded con- gregation upon the words: "That every tongue shall confess that Jesus Christ is lord, to the glory of God the Father." The thing that had always been the centre of the Chris- tian faith, he pointed out, was the belief in the supremacy of Jesus Christ. In times when that belief had faded the Church had been most powerless or most useless; and in times when this had been most implicitly be- lieved in the passion and devotion of the Church had been like a flaming fire. What was this lordship? There came One into, the world who, by this contact with the world and, with life and with men proved Himself lord and master—the supreme being. By the contact of Jesus Christ with the world he meant that war which the 'spiritual must always wage with the merely Material, and everybody knew that Jesus spoke- with authority, because He lived what He preached. There was no price at which His conscience could be bought; and the persecuting Pharisees—little men with the spirit of the world written all over them—could not under- stand Him who had no price. By His contact with life's experiences and His interpretation of them, Jesus Christ proved His lordship. There were three things we _could say about life—(l) that there was nothing.behind it at all, and that we were like bits of straw in the grip of a cataract, with no control over our own destiny; that was the emptiest and most hopeless philosophy a man could get hold of; or (2) that we might play the man and carry the burden put upon us although there was no good purpose in life; that was much nobler; it had a bit of back-

bone in it, although it was not Christian; or (3) that life had 1,1 meaning, and that death was not a blank gate, but an open country. That was the philosophy of Christ—and "every tongue shall confess that He is lord." And Reproved it by His contact with men, by the power He had of regenerating and re- creating them. That was the gist of the Gospel. And if the Church had lost it, it had ceased to be a Church, and we were only beating about the bush and wasting our time; we must learn from the Great Expert. He would make life true and good; that was the lordship of Jesus Christ. "A Dream of Heaven" was creditably taken by Miss Ethel Wells, the impressive solo parts being by the Misses Doris Travis and Ethel Wallington.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====

At all the services the choir and schOlars gave special hymns. The collections totalled 6s.—an increase of about .11 over those of last year.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
"A STILL HOLIER \VAR."

Alderman Bennett On The Work Of The Salvation Army.

"I hate the very name of war, but I regard the present struggle as a holy war, 'and our brave soldiers and sailors are fighting for prin- ciples which are absolutely essential to human freedom, civilisation and progress. But the Salvation Army are engaged in a still holier war, and as soon as they succeed in permeating all classes and races with the principles for which they stand, they will have made war im- possible and inaugurated the kingdom of heaven upon 'earth." In these words, Alderman Bennett, presiding at the anniversary proceedings of the local branch of the Salvation Army, at the Hall in Academy-street, on Sunday' afternoon, paid a tribute to the noble work of that vast force which carries on its beneficent operations in every part of the country and in many out- lying lands within the Empire and outside it.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
A MANY-SIDED UNDERTAKING.

Alderman Bennett said that he had always been greatly interested in the Work of the Sal- vation Army, and felt it a privilege to be asked to preside on the occasion of the 32nd anniver- sary, of the formation of the Warrington branch. He well remembered the meeting in the London Hippodrome, when the late General Booth delivered his last address to the public. He would never forget the great memorial ser- vice held at Olympia, or the final ceremony, when the dead General was borne through mil- lions of his admiring fellow 'men to his last resting-place in the Cemetery at Abney Park. He was greatly interested in the many-sidedness of the Salvation Army's work, and particularly at the present time, in their labours amongst the soldiers and sailors at home and abroad. He wished the Salvation Army every possible success in their splendid work, and cordially welcomed Brigadier Langdon back to Warring- ton, t 4,0 scene of his invaluable efforts nine- teen years ago.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
FOURTEEN THOUSAND SALVATIONISTS WITH THE COLOURS.

Brigadier Langdon delivered a most interest- ing address, largely anecdotal, dealing with the current work of the Salvation Army, and mak- ing particular reference to what is being done for our soldiers at home and on the Continent. The Brigadier, stated at the outset that between thirteen and fourteen thousand Salvationists were with the colours—a thousand of that num- ber from the Army's social institutions, men who were physical and moral wrecks when they came to the Army. Good food, regular work, and kindness had changed those men from being waste material, and a drag on the nation, into a fighting force. They were re-made men. Helping at Home and Abroad.

In Holland, officers of the Salvation Army visited the interned warriors; homes of rest and hostels had been opened in various centres in France, from which over ten thousand gar- ments, made and presented by the Women's Home Leagues in this country, had been distri- buted to the men. Free access had been given the Salvation Army officers to all British mili- tary hospitals in France, and a regular system of vi§itatiori was being carried on. A Naval and Military Institute was open in Liverpool from 11.30 p.m. to 9.30 a.m. for men stranded in the city, too late to catch trains to other parts, and over a thousand men had been cared for during the five reeks it had been open. A, dozen motor ambulances were manned by Salva- tion Army drivers and orderlies. The Hon. Arthur Stanley, M.P. (chairman of the Execu- tive Committee of the 8.R.C.5.), on his return from a visit of inspection to the battle area, made their work the subject of special praise in the Press. About two hundred officers were engaged at something like a hundred and twenty military centres up and down the land. Over ten thousand wounded Belgian soldiers had been received into the Salvation Army homes in various parts of the country. Adjutant Beamont-, who is in charge of the Warrington branch, proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman. TWENTY RECRUITS OBTAINED.

At the evening service a striking feature was the enrolment of twenty recruits, the majority of whom were transfers from the Sunday school.

A well-attended service was also held in the morning, when Bandsman J. Payton presented a Bible for platform use. The whole of the anniversary celebrations, which were concluded on Monday evening with a musical festival, at which badges for long ser- vice were presented to a* number of bandsmen and officers, were conducted by Brigadier and Mrs. Langdon, who twenty yearp. ago were sta- tioned in charge of the Warrington corps, but who now control the Liverpool Division, of which Warrington forms a part. They were heartily welcomed on Saturday evening by a large gathering, including many of those with whom they were associated in their earlier work here. At that meeting a new flag, obtained by means of subscription amongst the members, was presented to the corps.

APPEAL FOR A WALKING DAY.

• Sir,—Kindly allow me, through your paper, to make a special appeal for a Witlking Day. If there is not one there will be a large num- ber of poor children whose parents will not make any effort to provide them with boots and clothing. The tradespeople will suffer, as well as the children, as the money will be spent in a direction which will benefit neither. Some Saturday could be chosen and arrange- ments made whereby the procession could be over by half-past eleven. No time need be lost at the works, and the shops and Market could be opened as usual. The men at the Front want the children to have their usual Walking Day. Bewsey-street

===== 0002601_19150605_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOULD MR. ?REDMOND HAVE

JOINED THE CABINET?

Sir,—Since a Coalition Cabinet has now becln formed, one often hears it suggested that the Leader of the Nationalist party should have become one of its members. Irishmen, also have asked themselves the question whether or not the Irish party have acted in the best in- terests of Ireland. Personally, I have no hesita- tion in saying that the right course has been chosen siace the constituencies of , Ireland have so decided. The Irish party, in their wisdom, have decided upon and adopted an independent course—though such an adoption is bound to expose them to a new campaign of misrepresentation in England, However, the firm line drawn by the supporters of the Irish party will remain unaltered, and Such a policy will call a further rally to the unconquerable • spirit of Irish Nationality. Mr. Redmond has not taken a position in the Cabinet and one is relieved to find there is no longer any question on the point. There is only one post he could possibly have ac- cepted—the Irish Chief Secretaryship—and from all accounts he was never offered this office. The "Morning Post's" suggestion that he might have taken the Post Office with a ' seat in the Cabinet is not likely to cause any heart-burnings in Ireland, but there was a considerable feeling in favour of Mr. Red- mond's accepting the Chief Secretaryship—had he been offered it. But assuming he did so, one like myself, would consider such a move a tactical mistake. Certainly ist would have placed him in a strong position in Dublill Castle, but it would have ended the inde- pendent policy of an organisation that has been in existence too many years to fail. It is to big a risk to burn our own as well as other people's boats Many people have already assumed that the Tories have come back to power—indeed manY Irish people adhere to this opinion. Mr. Red- mond is out and Sir Edward Carson id! in the Cabinet. However, Mr. Redmond appears to hold the I stronger position. What do the Ulster covenanters think of their leader ac- cepting office in a "Home Rule" Government? What would Mr. Redmond's followers have said had he joined a coercion Government? Sir Edward Carson is now so compromised that his opposition to any Home Rule measure is half-destroyed. But what of Mr. Redmond? Though not damning the Empire in the House of Commons Buffet, he stands aloof from an experiment which may fail. He is the spokes- man of a party who do not consent to Parlia- ment being stampeded by newspapers into a revolutionary change. To-day Mr. Redmond stands in a position to exact the price of his support. and the cOali- tion Government (including Sir Edward Car- son) should be called upon to decide the posi- tion with regard to Home Rule. It should not he assumed that there is any "want of patriotism" in my script, but should there be even a tittle of doubt I may rightfully ask the critics to turn to Dr. Johnson.

A . 14RITISHER.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
SOMEHOW GOOD.

• ALFRED TENNYSON. Oh, yet we trust that somehow good Will be the final goal of ill, To pangs of nature, sins of will, Defects of doubt, and taints of blood;

That nothing walks with aimless feet; That no one life shall be destroyed, Or cast as rubbish to the void, When God bath made the pile complete;

That not a worm is cloven in vain; That not a moth with vain desire Is shrivelled in a fruitless fire, Or but subserves another's gain.

Behold, we know not anything; I can but trust that good shall fall At last—far off—at last, to all, And every winter change to spring.

So runs my dream; but what am I? An infant crying in the night: An infant crying for the light: And with no language but a cry.

"DRY, ROT" IN THE NAVY.

By C. E. PARKER, in

It was my luck to find in an old bookshop in our town a little book entitled " The.Tan- ners' Key," by one John Burridgo, published in 1824. The language of this old writer is somewhat flamboyant and very patriotic. If we may credit all ho tells us, he did a. great deal for the advancement of the tanning in- dustry in his day. He is entitled to the re- spect of tanners, because be introduced the useful instrument called the barktrometer for measuring the density of tanners' liquors. Burridge puts a " t," in the word after the " k," why, Ido not know. He says, " The simple instrument I use is an hydrometer, which I have surnamed barktrometer,' with- out which I should be more in the dark than brewers without saccharonieters or thermome- ters."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
A CALAMITY HOWLER.

Burridge was in advance of his time, and the book is of general interest because of its frequent reference to dry rot, not in. the tanyard, bñt in the wooden walls of the Navy. This appears, according to &midge, to have been in danger of destroying British warships quicker - than the country could build them. He tells us that 969 ships were destroyed be- tween the years 1814 and 1820 by dry rot. The cause of this, according to Burridge, is that the oak trees used in naval construction were felled in autumn before the sap had descended; in other words, they were built of sappy wood. Burridge goes on to say that in 1814 the whole British Fleet consisted of 1,040 vessels, estimated at 862,898 tons, and in 1820 of. 510 vessels, estimated at 520,287 tons' a decrease in vessels, ships of the line, and frigates, of 530, equal to 342,611 tons. _

A law, making it compulsory to fell eel in winter, was passed in the reign of Jame 7 1., 1605; this appears to have been annulled in 1792, when the building of ships with sappy oak came in. It seems strange that it was left to an obscure tanner to call the attention of the country to this.

Burridge found the Government of the day very slow to move, and it roused his indigna- tion to boiling point. " Surely," he writes, " a more ,Vital question never excited the de- liberation )of His Majesty's Cabinet," and he adds: " In consequence of having lately sur- veyed the Nelson," Rodney," Waterloo,' etc., etc., in Portsmouth Harbour, which are dry rotten, and the Navy in general in a de- plorable. state, defying description, I hpve resolved to publish all the facts."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
TANNERS IN DARKNESS.

The author appears to have other interests than that of tanning and the " dry rotten " Navy, for be promises to publish an essay on civil architecture ". with a view to prevent the dry rot on terra firma, which will embrace a new' and original system ,of architecture from the foundations to the roofs, by cheap, plain, and simple methods, not ingredients, for no nostrum under .heaven was, is, or ever will be found to cure the complaint, which resembles .mortifica Lion." The meaning, of this is not very clear, but if this essay was ever published, it would be well if it could be unearthed, as we all want to know how " to build houses from foundations to the roofs, by cheap, plain and simple methods." Burridge seems to have suffered through the .natural conservatism of the tanners (A his day, and lie goes for them in right royal style My humble attempt has naturally been regarded incredible by old tanners, who are likely to die with all their prejudices affixed to grey hairs. Tanners in general are lost in universal darkness, where they trust to their own eyes' more than commonsense." And again_:— _ • _

" For with a few exceptions, I never dealt with such miserable, illiberal, sluggish, illiter- ate bigots as tanners." " Let old tanners

===== 0002601_19150605_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHILDREN'S PART.

How 'this Year's Maypole Dancers have Helped.

. . In war time it is particularly gratifying to see the children dancing round the Maypole. It is gratifying, because for the nonce one is transported from a world where all is chaos and seemingly hbpeless strife to another world where the things that grown-ups think import- ant are relegated to a back place, and entirely different considerations hold sway. Yet, there are times when the young folk are almost re- proachful in their practical earnestness. • We have all seen the little ones gambolling around their Maypoles this year, and maybe we have felt personally grateful for the symbol of good, cheer and promise of better times. What a lot of its don't know is that many of these, young patriots have, all the time they have romped and gavotted round the Maypole, been "doing their bit," if the threadbare hut never worn out phrase, may be permitted.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
£l3 from Maypoles.

Nearly. £lO has been raised by the Misses Elsie Slater and Eva Amos and other little folk, friends of theirs, by giving Maypole dances in the streets of Warrington, and the money has .been handed over to the Warrington Infirmary and the Soldiers' and Sailors' Funds. Elsie Slater, who is only thirteen, lives at 14, Oxford-street, and Eva Amos, the same age, lives at 41, Slater-street. After calling together a - number of . other little children, they decided to act as Morris dancqrs, and had a proeession on Saturdays during the ..month of May. The actors in this little play were: Elsie Slater, Red Cross nurse (collector); Eva Amos, Red Cross nurse (collector); Lucy Brownridge (7), Queen; Mabel Brownridge (12), Britannia; Florence Reynolds. (8), gipsy ; Doris Amos (10), 80-peep; Dolly Rigby (11),

ar (i.4)8 Jester; Lily e , oValley; Mona Rigby 'On, Japanese; Dora Mitchell (12), Red Riding Hood; Polly Rigby (10), flower girl; Toni Rigby (5), soldier; Hugh Rafter (10), sailor; Albert Brimelow (10), standard-bearer.

In a similar way £3 has been collected by children trained by Mrs. Lewis, of 9, Rob- son-streei, and in this instance the, proceeds have been allocated to the local War Prisoners' Fund, of which Air. John Fairhurst is treasurer. This fund is for the benefit of Warrington sol- diers who have been taken prisoner by the enemy. Mrs. Barlow. of 13, Robson-street, has had charge of this collection. •

This solicitude of the children should put to shame those grumblers who do not yet appear to have realised that the nation is at war, but areP always grumbling because prices are "up."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 5, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
" The Leather AA Torld."

resign,- for they have been stumbling blocks too long." How would this do for the editors of our leather trade papers of to-day, I wonder?

Burridge appears to anticipate a shortage of bark. for tanning, and says: " Under these circumstances, .any substitute is a desidera- tum of vital importance to save Britannia's Tree of Liberty," and so his " next attempt was to seek foreign remedies against that national misfortune to preserve Britannia."

One of these remedies was found in a pro- duct of the East Indies called Terra Japoriica, or, as it is now named, Gambier, and our author in speaking of it says: " Hence the most- providential effects are realisable—our royal and commercial navies may be saved from the fatal dry rot, to hand our constitu- tion down to posterity."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADE IMPROVEMENTS.

There are several improvements in tanning and tanyards which Burridge suggests that give au indication of the condition of the industry at the time he wrote. Our author entertains a worthy ambition and expresses it as follows .

' And I have ambition to hope, that (after my halal, which now celebrates the dawn of tanning, shall have mouldered into dust) my plans, with many improvements, will be in general execution, without proving an un- worthy legacy to posterity." Among the improvements suggested may be named grinding of bark by windmills and steam engines instead of horses; the pro- aitessive strengthening of liquors as the goods get older, Fin d as .a result "reducing to months instead of years the tanning of a hide " ; (he adds, " this resembles a miracle I") ; the abolition of the vexatious excise regulations that hides should be tanned as a. whole (as is now our custom), and not rounded, Burridge also advocated; the sus- pension of hides in pits (but curious to say lie recommends: the suspension, • not in the early stages, but. in the later) ; the roofing in of tan- neries—which appears very exceptional at the time he writes, and the abolition of the legal regulation that a tanner should not also be a currier. _ _

Burridge also appeared to fancy that in his day carriers and facfors gained wealth with wgood deal less trouble than the poor tanners —an opinion shared by sonic in our own day. He advocates the repeal of the leather tax— I think be means the excise duty—because of the trouble and expense in the collection, and because " the officers in all weathers must 'gallop over the country to ovisit every distant tanyard daily." " How often,". be says, "do we not behold our fellow creatures traversing turnpike roads, like forlorn pilgrims, raggedly, nay nakedly, clothed, and bare- footed, trampling on Hints." This, I. sup- pose, he would hope to do away with by cheapening • leather and by doing away with the excise duty. The duties at this time for the importation of tanning matters were: Oak bark, 3d. per ewt.; valonia, 2s. 6d. cwt.; sumach, is. 7d. per cwt.; terra japonica, 3s, per cwt.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
A SWEEPING SUGGESTION.

I do not know bow Burridge would view the efforts of the tanners to insure better flayed, hides,. but he gives us an item of 4 good deal of interest when he says: " vexatious Act of Parliament was repealed last session respecting hides and skins which sub- jected butchers to various fines for cutting, gashing, or flawing of the smite, as if every man was regardless or his own property."

While he approves the repeal of this Act, he goes on to tiny : " l'here ought to be an Act of Parliament to compel tanners to follow your rules and . prevent people from acting like fools." Bravo, Burridge A most excellent idea—but would it work ?

Ait Tuesday night's meeting, of the Warrington Town Council the Bridges Committee presented a minute in which they desired to record their entire satisfaction with the able manner in which the engineers (Messrs. Figures which had been given him that .after- noon by the Borough Treasurer showed that the bridge had cost £25,523. " They had heard a, great deal about Messrs. Thorne's contract only costing them £20,000, but the Amount he, had mentioned was the total cost. Local Conditions 96 Years Ago,

To the Editor of " The Warrington Examiner."

Sir,--" Baines' History of -LancashirC " is not a rare book, but few of those who possess a copy probably ever Saw the original edition which was published in 1824-5 by Wm. Wales and Co., " Advertiser " Office' Liverpool. Through the kindness of a friend a copy of this is before me. /

In its original form the work embraces a directory as well as the history, and the former furnishes ihformation as to what may be termed the every-day life of Warrington, which, I think, will be interesting -to your readers.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
Coaches.

Standing astride,. as it were, of the main road connecting Liverpool and Manchester, it is scarcely to be imagined that Warrington would be likely to he in any sense a " sleepy hollow," and Baines tells us that the streets were in a state of perpetual animatimi by reason of the great -number of coaches pass- ing through the town. The directory gives a list of thirty coaches which bowled back- wards and forwards through the town daily, and wns is apart from local coaches arriving from neighbouring. towns on market days, Sze. There was, I believe, an hourly service during the day in each direction between Liverpool and Manchester, and the list gives the names of eighteen coaches engaged in this service. There were also seven coaches in each direction London-Liverpool, four Liverpool-Birming4m, and one Manchester- Chester. The names of the first six coaches in the sLiterpool and ;Manchester service are " Balloon," " Doctor.," " Mike," " Enter- prise," "Independent," and " Persever- ance," while to Birmingham I find the " Bang Up " and " locket and Magnus." One won- ders whether the latter might have suggested to Stevenson the name of his historic locomo- tive.

The. posting-houses were the " George," " Nag's Head," " Talbot," and " White Hart." The " Nag's Head " was evidently the leading house, as its patronageseems to have equalled that of the other, three com- bined.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
Packet Boats

In addition to these coaching facilities, there were also packet boats to Manchester and Runcorn, those of the " Old Quay Com- pany" embarking and disembarking passen- gers at Warrington Bridge .and the Black Bear Bridge, Latchford. There was a similar service on the Br. idgewater Canal, the packets on this taking on and putting off Warrington passengers at Stockton Quay. There is , a note intimating that the Runcorn service con- nected with the steam packets from Liverpool "according to tides." We have no particu- lars as to the coaching fares, although these would have been ,interesting for comparison with the railway fares of to-clay, but we do find that a voyage by packet in the " best cabin " to Manchester cost 2s. 6d., and to Runcorn Is. Passengers contented with less luxurious quarters might travel in the." after cabin" for. Is. 6d. and Od. respectively. The passages • occupied about six hours to Man- chester and two to Runcorn. Salmon Plentiful.

Whilst_dealing with this part of the subject we might note what is said as to the fisheries on the Mersey : In primitive times there was a valuable fishery in the Mersey. . . .

As late as 1763 the value of, this fishery was estimated at £4OO a year, but the perpetual disturbance and depredations to which the fry is subject have greatly reduced the num- ber of salmon, and the fine-flavourvd smelts or sparling,s which annually in spring came up the river in shoals, are now so greatly dimin- ished both in number and size, as to render the fishery much less! an object Of economy and luxury to the ,inhabitants of modern, than it was to those of ancient, .Warrington." In the text Baines places a "dagger against the word " salmon in the above sentenek, and adds this footnote: " At this period it was usual to insert a clause in indentures of apprenticeship at Warrington, by which the masters stipulated not to oblige their appren- tices to eat salmon more than twice a week." Manufactures.

In reference to the town's manufactures the author says: " Coarse linens and checks formed the first manufactures of this place; this gave way. to a species of table linen called huckaback ; which was succeeded by strong sailcloth (poldavy), of which Warrington is represented to have made as much at one time. as supplied half the British Navy. .

The breweiS-still produce fine Warrington ale from malt, for which this place has been long celebrated,"

It is impossible not to admire • the very thorough manner in which the old Warring- tonians made provision for dispensing this. " fine ale." The population of the " town- ship " (Warrington was not incorporated till 1847) is given as 13,571 (in 1821) .and the directory gives a list of 84 hotels and taverns. In the -adjacent dusty, coal-getting borough of Wigan, there were at the same date 68 houses to a population of 17,710, so that in this matter Warrington had gene one better than its neighbour, and we can hardly think. that an odd one out of the 13,571 tould be likely to encounter any difficulty in securing the wherewithal when he desired to slake his thirst', •

The author to, us : " The government of the town is vested in the constables, and these 9fficers act under the superintendence and direction of the ,magistrates of the Warring- ton division of the hundred of West Derby."

FAREWELL.

"Deeds that survive" was the title of the Wycliffe P.S.A. address at Sunday's meeting. It was given by Mr. William Street, whO said that actions which were mire and good left be- hind them an undying memory. Deeds of un- selfishness, deeds of helpfulness—these it Was that always came out "on top";. those of selfish- ness and sin sank lower, and we desired to for- get them. The greatest deed ever performed *as our Lord's self-sacrifice ;Ilion Calvary's Cross; He stooped and conquered. Pilate's effort to releage Christ, and the action of the dying thief -would also survive and be cherished. But the great redemption of men lived every day of our own' experience; Christ's deed satisfied the Eternal One

Messrs.' Yearsley and C. Healey' also *partici- pated in the service, whilst Mr. W. 11. Payton rendered in pleasing manner an organ solo. The Chairman' (the Rev. T. T. James) heartily welcomed Brother Vfollington—who having been wounded, was now on leave re- cuperating.—The Secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) alluded to the fact that the following Sunday (to-morrow) would be the last before Mr. James' departure for France—where he is to serve with the Y.M.C.A. for three months and announced that the meeting would be an oven one. A large assembly of ladies and gen- tlemen, he added, was invited and anticipated.

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

The Need For Care In Buying

Second-hand Machines.

A TERRIBLE HILL.

(Queries and correspondence should be addressed to "The Hub," c/o The Editor. Replies can- not be sent by poit and all queries will be answered in our columns.)

If the advertisement columns (A various pro- vincial and London papers are watched care- fully, it will be noticed that there always appears to be a large number of second-hand cycles for disposal. Of course, some of such advertisements are genuine, the owners really going abroad, forced to give up cycling by doctor's orders, or going in for a motor cycle or car. But very many of them are. purely fakes. Machines are specially _made for this trade; machines that are not .worth more than 30s. or 355., which they originally cost. The wiles of those who devote their. energies to this branch of the business are remarkably clever, and it is of little wonder that so many novices are taken in. This war has been the "sharks'" opportunity, since so many riders have been 'disposing of their machines: before going to the Front. - Judging by the number of advertisements that have appeared, how- ever, one can only believe that every man in the new Army must have been the fortunate possessor of two or three machines.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NAMED VARIETY.

If I were contemplating buying a second- hand cycle from such a source, one of the first things I would look for would be the name of the machine... In the second ,place, the size of the frame would require to be) stated. ' I have come across cases in which well-known makes Were advertised, 'but on writing the reply came back that such and such a cycle had 114 n sold, but, for some reason or another, the advertiser had another machine for dis- posal, which, though by a smaller maker, was equally good. This is where the "dud" cycle comes in. It -must be profitable to purchase a new' machine for, say, 305., and sell it ag a second-hand one for 4:4 and Upwards. Again, with reference to the question of the .size of the frame. This is never stated in a bogus advertisement. The owner endeavours ,to find out the requirements of the intending pur- Chaser when he writes giving the exact speci- fication which will meet the case. A word of warning to likely buyers is never to send money for a cycle unless perfectly sure that the machine is a genuine second-hand article made by 'a firm of repute. HOTEL RESTRICTIONS.

A cyclist and his wife called for -lunch at an inn in Surrey, but were turned , out part way through the meal as "travellers were not allowed on the premises after two o'clock." The cyclist took the matter up with the owners of the house. The following is the reply he has received, which we cull .from our contem- porary, "Cycling ": " I am very much Obliged to you for taking the trouble to write me with regard _to our

_ manager at the _ at -. He wag formerly a police-sergeant, and carries out all orders that he receives literally and without inquiry. The War Office had on that day issued an order to all licensed houses in the Aldershot training centre to close at two o'clock, and the order was so worded that on the face of it the manager was acting strictly in accordance with his instructions. I have had the matter in correspondence with General Sir Archibald Hunter, and he has now made

THE BICYCLE IN INDIA.

Although Germany made great efforts to get hold of the Indian cycle trade, she was singu- larly unsuccessful, except in the very cheapest rubbish,- of a class to which no British manu- facturer would attach his name. It is the maintenance of consistent high quality which has kept the Indian trade for the British, bi- cycle, and an illustration of this is afforded by Major de S. W. Passey of the 25th Punjaubs, who has been riding a Raleigh for many years. "It has never given me any trouble," he writes, "indeed the „ Raleigh has always (lone me well and I am very pleased with it." The maintenance of high quality has always been the -chief asset of the British maker, and it is an asset which improves in value as buyers get to know that they `I can rely upon the product of the first-elan British manufac- turer.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTRIC LIGHTING.

The vast improvements that have been made in recent years in accumulators now .render it possible for motor cyclists to fit an electric hand-light on their machines. Many fight shy of using such an outfit,- especially if they ex- perienced the failure of accumulator ignition in the early days: A modern dry battery can be used very successfully for such a purpose, and the .dost is very small indeed. A four-volt bulb, giving one candle-power is the best, and if there is a good reflector behind, a first-class light will be given. A 'Very important point is the bulb. Whenever this shows the slight- Vst sign of blackening a new one should be fitted. It is an excellent plan to have two batteries in use at the same time, working them _alternately, since this will prolong their lighting power. A battery costing 41d. should give some three hours' light if the wiring, &c., is in good order. The cost is not great, and the ,illumination given is quite satisfactory.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0251.txt | included=true | included =====
HILL-CLIMBING.

Judging by the roads that have been selected in the past for trials, I had come to the con- clusion that the worst.possible had been found. I made a mistake, however, for a terrible track has been .discovered. The latest is known as Moor End. It-is near Kettlewell, in Wharfe- dals,'Yorkshii.e. The old pack road rises 1,000 feet to the tableland of Old Cote Moor. A number of motor cyclists and light carists at- tempted the ascent, Tut only two of the former Were /successful in reaching the summit. It says much for the qualities of the riders and their mounts that they were able tq reach the top; it says .very little for the respect they have for their machines. I have often tramped over this road and the surface in places is nothing but a stone strewn track. The wear on the tyres and the strain on the engines must have been tremendous. -

===== 0002601_19150605_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR OUTPUT.

Mr. T. P. O'Connor, M.P., speaking as far back" as 1902, stated': "While automobilism may at present, in its higher refinement, be the. privilege of the rich, it will eventually be not a monopoly, not a pastime restricted to the affluent, few, but- a real, a great, and a permanent pleasure—even a blessing—to all classes." These words have come `true. One firm in America will make and sell 325,000 cars this year; in March last oh one day 2,096 cars were produced.' Between 23,000 and 24,000 Men are employed and the plant works for 24 hours U day, in three shifts. 'lke effect of this eon-Hit:lts et twit has been to I.;:ng motcr ears into the range of possibilities for nearly every- one. It is correct now that the motor car is for the million; not for the few who can afford to spend•hundreds on a luxurious (mgt. The production of a car and a half per minute is a marxellous achievement.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
LIGHT CARS AND SPEED.

Whenever light carists congregate two ques- tions always come to the f9re. These are the speed and the • petrol consumption. There is a very great difference between what a car can do and what she should be asked to 'do. That it is possible to get 45 m.p.h. from such a machine is quite correct; no one however, would derive • any great pleasure by travelling so quickly. Even with a first class surface a light car going all out covers the ground with a series of jumps. The car requires holding at this speed and all fittings rattle undulY°. The ideal is to drive at 30-5 m.p.h. keeping the remaining 10 m.p.h. to .be used only when emergency renders 4t desirable. Such a pro- cedure will add considerably tp the life of the car; it will .give more comfortable running; the. cost for petrol and tyres will be 'reduced, and the ground will be covered at a speed which is sufficient for all and ordinary pur- poses.

NOBODY'S CHILDREN.

ilo‘‘ Couneil School Ellocalion May Help To !tenon(' A Slignia.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0260.txt | included=true | included =====
kIIRINGTON GUARDIANS'

DECISION.

Nobay's children! It is a sad phrase; and it represents a problem. The Warrington Guardians were up against it on Wednesday, at their fortnightly meeting. The question of whether to send the Protestant children at the Iladgate Ccttage Homes to Council schools or cOntinue to educate them within the Poor Law premises, was the principal topic of dis- cussion. The Board eventually decided, by a large majority, in favour of the Council school.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0261.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RESOLUTION.

.pursuance of notice given at the last meet- _ Miss Broadbent- moved: "That Mrs. Alice Ether Lowrance be and she is hereby appointed supet:intendent and Matron of the Cottage Hoines and SchoOls, Padgate, at a salary after the rate of £75 per annum, together with resi- dencp, rations, and washing, subject to the sanc- tion of the Local Government, Board, the provi- sions of the Poor Law Officers' Superannuation Act, ,1896 (for which purpose the_emoluments are valued at £5O per annum), and one month's notice in writing on either side tuterminate the engagement." And further :" That as soon as the. Warrington,Council .School at Oakwood- averite is open for the reception of children, all thi Protestant children resident in the Cottage Homes of this Union, between the ages of seven anti fourteen, be sent thereto."

===== 0002601_19150605_art0262.txt | included=true | included =====
RUB SHOULDERS WITH OTHER CHILDREN.

Broadbent pointed out that the removal of the Roman Catholic children had reduced the Dumber of clilldr-en at the Homes to about half the original figures. They now woofed one .officer instead of two. The committee .felt that Mrs. 'Lowrance was in every way the woman for the job. She had not sat down and mourned since her husband died, but,- had set to, work and kept the place in splendid order in every way, winning the ,admiration not only of the members of the committee and others who had noticed the excellent condition of affairs at the Homes, but also of the Inspectors, who had re- ported with great satisfaction. She was a woman of great- ability, strength of character and re- finement. Personally, she (Miss Broadbent) ,felt sure that the Local Government Board would ratify the action of the Board in'appoint- ing $o 'excellent an officer to the post of super- intendent. She asked the Guardians most 'earnestly- not to show their sympathy with Mrs. Lowrance, but to show their appreciation of the excellent work she had (lone by supporting her heartily. With regard to the second part of the resolution, the committee felt it would be a far better thing for the children to go outside to school. In that way they rLixed with other children, which was very desirable.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0263.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN RATHER THAN MEN.

The Bev. A. M. Mitchell said that at this particular timo they should employ women in- stead of men wherever they could, so that more men would be free to serve their country with the colours, He did not think they should em- ploy any man under military age. For that reason alone he felt that the Board should appoint Mrs. Lawrance; no woman, he felt cer- tain, Could be found who would be more suited to the situation. There was no doubt, from the reports they had from the members of the com- mittee; and from H.M. Inspectors, that anhinflu- ence for good was at work at the Homes. He very strongly doubted whether, if the Board advertised,' they would get a woman of the general calibre of Mrs. Lawranee Ml' the same salary. Of course, women were greatly in de- mand just now, and they were showing what sort of stuff they were made of. .

A CASE FOR CAREFUL CONSIDERATION. Mr. Mather moved an amendment. The step that it was proposed they should take was one that should not uO. taken mu hurry. There was a great deal that wanted considering. For one thing, if the children were to be sent to the Council schools, consideration would have to be given to the time they had tUspend in going to and from school, in regard to tne matter of the work they did at the Homes. There would be a couple- of miles. each way for them to walk. Sand if they went home .to dinner, it would not only take,up a lot of time, but there was the 'consideration of the tiring nature .of the day. Thorough discussion was called for. All the evidence should be collected and a concise report be presented to the Beard. lie would move that a committee of three be appointed to collect the evidence., so that everything could be thoroughly sifted before the Guardians took a step that was revolutionary in its essence. Then there were rumours that as the war progressed more school buildings, would be required for wounded sol- diers, anfl it wlis probable that the Oakwood. avenue School would be one of them. Mr. Bent seconded the amendment.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0264.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FINANCIAL ASPECT.

Mr. Brooks said it was a stage in a 'great im- provement that was being brought about at the Cottage Homes. It was certainly revolutionary. The people who were in the best position to judge as to whether or. not the principle em- bodied in the resolution was a wise one to adopt were those. who were on the committee, and attended there regularly._ When the Catholic children were taken away a statement was made that. the Guardians were going to save several hundred pounds lier annum. lie contended that at least two hundred pounds would be saved by adopting the proposals of the committee. The cost of sending ,the sixty or seventy children concerned by the change to the Council schools Would 141 only: abaut £66, which when sub- tracted from the sum saved in salaries, left a balance of well over £2OO. From an educational point of view, there was a great deal to be said for the change. The value of an industrial school education was not etiuivalent. to that of the Council school education. By attending the Council school the children from the Hoines rubbed shoulders with other children, which Was a great advantage to them socially. Mr. McKinley Complained about the proposal that a committee be formed to go into the matter, as if the whole of the Hewes Committee was less capable than three members of the Board. Ile took the suggestion as a personal

===== 0002601_19150605_art0265.txt | included=true | included =====
FOLLOW THE CATHOLICS' EXAMPLE.

. . Shaw.snid he wanted to see. the children in. the Homes dealt with in such a way that they would not be pointed at in the future. He Wanted to see Meth grow up the same as other children; and to have the stigma that divided them .fronNther children removed for ever. They, as Protestant's, should follow the excellent

===== 0002601_19150605_art0266.txt | included=true | included =====
A RADICAL CHANGE.

'Mr. Dale said that the resolution before the meeting involved a radical change, and the whole question called for serious consideration as to whether the proposed alterations were going lo be to the advantage of those Concerned. Personally, he was not in favour of the trans- ference of the Catholic children to the Catholic school, but he was in favour of the present sug- gestion. He believed the adoption of the com- mittee's suggestion would materially advance the position of the Cottage Homes children in society. Those children could not help the con- dition into which they were brought, and he be- lieved that every member of the Board Was anxious to do .his best for them. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150605_art0267.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE INFORMATION WANTED.

The Vice-Chairman (Mr. W. Furness) depre- cated the suggestion of a member of the Homes Committee that that committee had been slighted. It was an 'important matter. The Board- had accepted the principle that the chil- dren should be sent away, but the financial aspect called for consideration. The committee ought to be prepared with a complete state- ment on the matter. The change that it was proposed to make should be justified from the moral, social and financial points of view, lie thought that the Board were entitled to ask for figures. They had not beett-taken into the con- fidence of the committee as they ought to have been.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0268.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLEGED DRIFTING.

The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) said ho did not think, on the whole, that the matter had received the careful consideration it should have. It seemed to him, as he had said before, that the Guardians were being :Asked to drift— as the committee had drifted—towards a revo- lutionary change that would involve consider- able expense. lie thanked Mr. Brooks for the figures, which were, he believed, quite correct, so far as they went. But it was not a complete financial survey of the consequential expense; that would accrue. If the financial .aspect of a complete well-thought-out scheme in the inter- ests solely of the children had been presented to the Board, it would probably have received their support. It would certainly have had his (the Chairman's) support. It appeared to him that a number of members Nod been more swayed by personal regard for Mrs. Lowrance than they should have been, although he did not want any- one to misunderstand him in that respect; he thought Mrs. Lowrance was more suited for the post than any other woman they might get. But personal considerations should not he allowed too much weight. If they had been boldly and plainly told that the sole reason for the proposed change was based on a desire to befiefit the children of the Homes, educationally and otherwise, and that there would be in- creased expense, he ventured to suggest that they would have faced it cheerfully. • He did not like a policy of drift. As one who had worked under the old system for a quarter of a century, and found it to answer well, he hesi- tated at voting for a drastic change without sufficient reason being forthcoming. He pro- tested against the suggestion that there was a stigma attaching to the system of educating the children themselves. They removed years ago all ground for any prejudices that might have existed in the matter of the education of the children at the Cottage Homes. If the Board passed the resolution he would do his utmost to see that the new system worked thoroughly and smoothly. He hoped, if the new system came to be adopted, that the Board would deal gener- ously with the problems of fresh expense that would arise.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0269.txt | included=true | included =====
CARRIED IT THROUGH LIFE.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said that it would not be right for the Guardians to debar the children in their care from mixing with other children. No stigma attached to them so far as that Board was concerned, and a large number of other people, but, no matter what Might be done when children were brought up under such condition,, there was always the institutional stigma. They ,carried it with them as they went through life. The resolution was put to the meeting and carried by thirteen votes to four.

GIRLS IN PUBLIC-HOUSES

Resolution Passed By Warrington

Board of Guardians.

The vexed question of the sending Of girls from the Cottage Homes to sitnations on licensed premises was once again brought up at Wednesday's meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians.

On the proposition of Mr. T. Dale, the Board made a definite decision not in future to send any girls into employment on any premises licensed for the sale of intoxicating liquor.' In pursuance of notice given at the last meet- ing, Mr. Dale now mowed: "That no girls from the Cottage Homes and Schools be sent into ser- vice or employment with any person resident en premises which are licensed for the sale of intoxicating liquor." In moving the resolution, Mr. Dale observed that it was not necessary for him to make a speech, as the matter had already been thor- oughly discussed. ' The Rev. A. M. Mitchell, in seconding the resolution, said that it only established what the Board had already given its consent to. There was a general impression amongst the members of the Board that there was a resolu- tion to the effect that 110 girl from the Homes should go to work on licensed premises, and that there should be no exception, no matter how strong a case might be made out for any indi- vidual applicant., The Board had been acting up to a tradition—and a very good tradition— for years past The mind of the Guardians was, he thought, in favour of the Board dealing with the girls in their charge in a way such as they would deal with their own children. CARRIED BY ONE VOTE. •

A vote was taken, and there voted fOr the, reso- lution: Miss Broadbent, the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, the Chairman, Messrs. Mather, Dale, Billipger, Furness and Hughes. Against :"MrS. Hall, Messrs: Bent, Brooks, Shaw, Seton, Flan- agan and McKinley. TwO members did not vote.

COMFORTS FOR SOLDIERS.

Women's Liberal Association Still

Arth C'

The Women's Liberal Association sewing and knitting meeting is still being held at the John Crosfield Memorial Hall.

Substantial consignments of comforts of various kinds have been sent this week to the Drill Hall for the Territorials, to the Parr Hall for the prisoners of war in Germany, and to, individual Warrington men in all parts. Mrs. Osborne, the honorary secretary of the Association, has received many acknowledg- ments from recipients. Private E. Thomas, stretcher-bearer in the Territorials, says how delighted he was to receive a. parcel of the things lie required most—shirt and socks—" as one is obliged to change oftener now the warm weather is on. The tads here are looking remarkably fit and I never felt better in my life." On the other hand, Driver Wilkes, of the Territorials, says the Consignment was very welcome as there had been plenty of rain, and lie had been wet through—" and it is a relief to know that one has a warm change." Private Ford says his parcel arrived just as he was leaving the trenches after fifteen days "so you can guess what a clean change was to. me."

Private F. Dickenson, of the King's Own Scottish Borderers, in training, said the change came in handy because "we went out on Wednesday morning till Thursday night, taking•trenches." Gunner M. Graham, E.G.A., writing from the South of England, thinks that if the young men of Warrington knew how "you ladies were -treating the -soldiers they would all don the khaki and do • their hit."

The Importance of Accessories. -Accessories gain rather than lese in impor- tance ILS the days go by. A revival that should rejoice the heart of the elderly woman is the Victorian palerine of silk or lace, that crosses in front, comes to the elbows, .and falls in long stole ends in front. It is edged with one or two frills of silk or lace, and is furnished with a deep collar, also frilled. At the back it has the appearance of a small cape. The Toby frill of pleated tulle or chiffon, or organdi muslin, is •another notewortlig return. One charming model consisted of a wide piece of velvet that fitted closely to the neck. Under the chin and ears was a delicate frill of tulle, and from the opposite edge of the neck-band two dainty, deep frills of the tulle formed a fascinating capelet that. covered the shoulders, and fastened in front under a row of tiny black 'velvet buttons. Such a. frill could be made by the merest tyro for Et shilling or two. With these Toby frills -the bell-shaped Early Victorian sleeve in chiffon or some other trans- parent fabric accords admirably. The old- fashioned .beaded handbag of the Dorothy type is again to the fore, and generally repeats the colouring of the toilette it accompanies.

Bathing Dresses for the Beach. Bathing dresses become every season prettier and More becoming yid this year those de- signed for the beach rather than for swimming and exercise in the water, follow the lines of the new fashions, and flare out at the sides. They consist of knickerbockers and a dress in one, piece, neither of which covers the knees. The necks are round or pointed, ac,_.erding to taste; the dress fastens on the shoulders:and is slipped on over the bead. The Princess style is largely adopted, but 'intiny bther de- signs are seen. One model had rathel• a deep,, straight yoke, which supported pleats arranged after the manner of a Norfolk, coat. The neck .and short sleeves were trimmed with .a plaid fabric, and a sash of the same was worn quite low on the waist line. The knickevs are more comfortable when joined to a simple

loose under-bodice. Taffetas, more especially in black, is first favourite for this style, of suit, but pear de soie and mohair are also used. The high collar at the back appears on some models. '

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information in Popular Language:

Kidneys

Is any particular diet better than another for the kidneys i• Does frequent bathing lessen the work of the kidneys and is any certain form of exercise good for these .organs? Drink plenty of water and eat foods contain- ing .comparatively small quantities of sugar. Bathing of the body relieves the hidneys to a certain extent, as more of the refuse matter of the blood might then be exhaled through the skin. Walking is the. best exercise for flue kidneys.

Clean 'Hands and Teeth

There is the matter of clean hands. 'Alw,t mothers see to it that the baby's dimpled on have the appearance of cleanliness and that the older children wash before meals. But more titan this is necessary for safety. Bands become contaminated so easily and so often and in so many ways that they need to be very frequently wasinsd. The clean hands and clean finger-nails habit would save multitudes of children's lives. Keeping the fingers away from the lips, the nose and the eyes would

likewise save many an attack of illness. All- over cleanliness of the body ought: to become a personal habit with every child. Clean teeth is another habit that all parents should feel a responsibility in inculcating into their • chil- dren's lives.

Uric Acid.

The beginnings of .sitch apparently diveise ailments at diabetes, Bright's disease, apoplexy, epilepsy, asthma, aneurism, .pneunkonia and a score of others are by some medical men attri- buted to the presence in the blood or in other tissues of nit excess of uric acid. Be that as it may, this Compound is undoubtedly one of the main factors in the production of the more prominent and painful symptoms of gout and rheumatism. It gets into our bodies in the fopn of those. foods to which we have be- come most accustomed, and therefore like best. Butcher's meat, poultry, fish, game; eggs, peas, beans, tea, coffee—such are the foods richest in uric acid or its equivalents. But even the pure vegetarian cannot escape the presence of this fatal and perfidious chemical. The oxidation of his own flesh results in the production of a certain, if limited,. amount of the deleterious acid, but he certainly escapes the penalty of an excess.—Dr. Robertson • Wallace, in the "Ladies' Field," •

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By -MARJORIE.

The Women's War Hospitals. The Women's Hospital Unit originated in Scotland, Pr. Elsie Inglis being chiefly respon- sible for its formation. The first •unit was sent to lloyaumont, where it met with a rather cool reception from the French medical authorities. They, however, were very soon convinced of the pompetency of both women doctors and nurses,' and the result is that the military hospital at • Royaumont, staffed entirely by women, has been placed on exactly the same footing as those controlled by men. One of the .Scottish units has been, and still is, doing magnificent work, for Serbia. A second unit despatched to- that brave little country was detained, at Malta, where wounded soldiers from the • Dardanelles, are nursed. Other units 'ate no's 'being formed, such as the London Oul Welsh, lint all will telong to the Scottish Women's War Hospitals.

40:0 Miss Hilda Cowan's Dolls. . The war has caused a revolution in toyland, and the grotesque and ugly plaything is fortu- nately going out of fashion with the cessation of the German imports. Artists of repute have turned their attention to dolls and ani- mals, with the greatest success. Their pro- ductions are altogether delightful, and cannot fail to implant a love of the beautiful in the occupants of the nursery. Tire dolls modelled on the children depicted by Miss Hilda Cowan were quite a feature of the British Industries' Fair at the Agricultural Hall. Miss Dunn, a well-known painter of animals, has lately held an exhibition of the most realistic toys, which, she has produced in conjunction with Miss de Bude, another clever artist. Horses, dogs, birds, in fact a complete Noah's Ark, were shown, and each was an exact representation in colour of the living animal.

Woman's Opportunity. . The police will not license women taxi-cabi drivers in London, but women are. taking the, places of men in various branches of the rail- way and tramway services. The persistent call for men is woman's opportunity, and there will soon be no fewer than • twenty thousand policewomen employed in this country. A very little training suffices for many of the

lIONIE NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

To Clean Books.

Cloth uovered books having betomn soiled through handling can be cleaned .by rubbing them with bread crumbs, which use freely. If the marks still remain use very soft rubber. If there are any grease spots put a piece of blotting paper over the spot and apply a hot iron. •

To boil Macaroni. Macaroni or spaghetti should lw boiled in the same way as rice, namely, thrown into boiling salted water, and should never under any circumstances be soaked or placed in cold water previously. It should be tested occa- sionally with a fork, and when tender a tea- cupful of cold water should be thrown into the pan to stop the boiling; the pan should.. then be lifted from the tire, the macaroni or spag- hetti drained of all liquid, and returned to the hot dry pan and kept hut until wanted. The time for boiling is. from twenty to thirty minutes. Spaghetti Puouing.

Cook 4 oz. of spaghetti as previously directed, drain it, and place half of it in a pie-dish, grate 2 oz. of cheese (a good cooking one should be chosen), and sprinkle some of it over the spaghetti; slice four or five small tomatoes, and place them on the bed of spaghetti, sprink- ling them with cheese, pepper, and salt, and finish with the remainder of the spaghetti, placing the'rest of the cheese on the top. Pour carefully over this half a pint of milk; and cover the dish with a plate or small dish; cook in -a moderate oyn. A fire-proof dish with a fitting cover would be useful for this dish, but if not -procurable a pie-dish can quite well be used. •• •

A Novel Use For Thin Sheets. Sheets which have worn themselves very thin are not .any use as sheets or anything. which requires real wear and tear, and it is an economical plan to "cream" them with a penny tint, and use for curtains for the bed- They will last quite a long while, and be really more useful as curtains than made over into pillowslips, &c. Personally, I think it wasting time to put much work into thin sheets, &e. If they can be used as they are for curtains or something similar they are worth keeping, but otherwise they are not of much use, although if not very thin they can be converted into bags of all descriptions;

Dear Food.

Farmers and stock raisers may be troubled about shortage of labour, but in other ways they must be having the time 'of their lives. With bullocks fetching X5O, and fat sheep selling for 15 a-piece, the profits on British- fed meat must be enormous, notwithstanding the increased .cost of feeding-stuffs. .Very high prices are also being obtained for grain and produce. of all kinds—such prices, in fact, as have scarcely been known within living memory. . Whilst,- however, agriculturists are prospering the middle and lower class house- wife is faced. with a state of things that. she can only adequately describe as " awful." The Government has appealed to people to eat less meat, and, at last, they will be compelled to, for we are told that :prices, instead of falling, will go still higher.' There has been just as serious a.falling-off in,the supply of -fish, as of imported meat, but the Government, it is stated, are taking measures to increase--the fish supplies. The Vogue of Navy. Blue.

Linen is to play a very important part in this summer's scheme of dress and in colours will prove a formidable rival to cotton crepe and voile. In navy blue it will be at its best. The popularity Of this colour is astonishing., Many of the best dressed women bear toilettes in which no 'other shade appears.. in France. the same thing is noticeable, and Frenchwomen are seriously Considering the advisability of adopting navy blue for mourning. Should they do so, it is. quite probable that the cus- tom will be followed in other countries, it has been already decided that dark colours are to be donned next 'Winter, and among the shades selected blue ranks first. .Black and white, purple, dark red, green and mud-brown follow. „Contrasting touches of colour are often very cleverly introduced on , a navy blue tailored or other suit, and a soft shade of

cover-all aprons for bed-making (it is essential that a clean apron should be kept to wear when making beds, otherwise if a somewhat Foiled one is worn the beds are, apt to become dirty). The above suggestions do not, of course, apply to linen sheets, which are use- ful, no matter how old they may .be, for ,all kinds of things, as even a small piece can be convnted into a d'oyley centre, the better parts for a drawn-thread duchesSe set, &c. "Maud," in "Farm and Home." . Savoury R ice.

With' meat at its present high prices, the fol- lowing nourishing dish is likely to be appre- ciated by the economical, housewife: Place in a greased pledish layers of cooked—well cooked— rice, and slices of tomatoes, seasoned with pepper and salt, till dish is full. Orate some hard cheese thickly on top, add some small knobs of butter, and a little stock, and bake till brown. Serve very hot.

The work'people themselves could make the application, apart from the employers, and if, say, three or four girls working in the one pike made application on their own, it would be granted, so long as they came in one body. The MAYOR, detailed the terms which had been decided upon for the use of the baths by batches of boys and girls between the ages of 14 and 15.

Councillor BROADHURST resented the use of the word deplorable, as applied to the discust,iion by Councillor Wilkinson, and said 'Oat members of the Council had a•perfect right to know what was taking place, adding " I can put the gloves on as well as Councillor Wilkinson—big as he is—and don't let him forget it." lie was not there to give preferential treatment to the em- ployes of any firm; be was there for justice for every boy and girl.

SATURDAY'S WINNERS.

Saturday's winners were :—F. Lawless (Firth Mattress Co.); R. Straw (Greening's); W. Drury (Cheshire Lines); H. Gatley (Richmond's); J. Howarth (Naylor's); H. Hayes (Cheshire Lines); E. Hardman (Firth Mattress Co.); M. Durhan (Walker's Brewery); P. Wright (Crosfield's); A. Gleave, jun. (Crosfiela's); H. Cook (Crosfield's); A. Dunbobbiu (County Asylum); R. Smith (County Asylum); J. Pro- bert (Pearson and Knowles); W. Meakin (Pearson and Knowles); J. Norris (White- cross). TIPS FOR COMPETING TRUNDLERS.

The above named, together with the winners on succeeding Saturdays, will play off for the prizes. As far as possible 64 games will be played off each Saturday, and the players who emerge successfully from their first round are requested to stay on the green, so that they may play their second round games, and, if successful, their third round ties also. Owing to the number of entries it is essen- tial that no time should be wasted, and, to that end, if a player who has entered is not forth- coming within reasonable time of his name being called, he is disqualified. To-day 128 entrants are due to meet, the first tourney being timed for two o'clock and the remaining five at intervals of 20 minutes.

LOCAL TEAMS IN COL\ 'IV COMPETITIONS.

In the draw for the third round of the Championship Competition in connection with the Lancashire County Bowling Association, which took place at the Albion Hotel, Man- chester, on Monday night, the Warrington Bull's Head Club drew a bye. In the draw for the second round of the Association Competition Warrington Cemetery were drawn against Leigh Railway, the match to be played on the ground of the Wigan Ltd. Club.

In the third round of the Second Teams' Competition Warrington Cemetery were drawn to meet Radcliffe Withens, on the Stretford Angel green; whilst in the second round of the Second Teams' Consolation Competition Warrington Bull's Head are due to meet Leigh Liberals, at Kersal,, the match to be played on June 12th.

Opening Carnes In Nlonstre War Fund Handicap.

The War Fund Handicap arranged by the Warrington Works' Bowling Association was commenced on the Horse and Jockey Green, Winwick-road, on Saturday in moderate weather and in the presence of a goodly num- ber of trundling enthusiasts. As was an- nounced last week an excellent entry was se- cured, but the man who makes up his mind slowly has still an opportunity of competing for one of the excellent prizes on offer—and at the same time of rendering slight assistance to it deserving object, whilst also getting some real excitement and enjoyment for himself— for the entry list will not be closed until June 12th.

First -Round of Club Championship.

The first round of the Club Championship in connection with the Warrington and Dis- trict Amateur Bowling Association was played on Satnrday afternoon. The weather, though passable, was scarcely ideal for the business in hand, but, nevertheless, the various teams concerned managed to complete their fixtures. Six tourneys were played, and in four instances victory was secured by pretty substantial margins. The Senior Conservatives fairly ran away from the Rope and Anchor combination, winning by no fewer than 150 points, every member of the victorious team coming off best in his rubber. Rylands's No. 1 accounted for the Stag Hotel (Walton) combination in fairly easy manner, finishing 94 to the good; and the Bank Quay Bowling Club disposed of Walker's Brewery with 88 in hand. Penketh Tannery were too good for the Bull's Head (Church- street) team, though the margin in this case was the moderate one of 29, and the closest game of the afternoon was that between the Cemetery Hotel and the Junior Conservatives, only a dozen points dividing the two teams. Rylands's No. 2 combination had to give way to the Earlestown Vulcan dozen by 61 points. The scores will be found detailed below

Was Banana the Forbidden Fruit?

The banana is believed by some people to be the original forbidden fruit of the Garden of Eden. In any case, it is one of the curiosities of the vegetable kingdom, being not a tree, a palm, a bush, a shrub, a vegetable, or an herb, but a herbaceous plant with the status. of a tree. Although( it sometimes attains a height of 30 feet, there is no woody fibre in any part of its structure, and the bunches growing on the dwarf banana plant are often heavier than the stalk which supports them.. No plant gives such a quantity of food to the acre as the banana; it yields 44 times more by weight than the potato, and 133 times more than wheat. Moreover, no insect will attack it, and it is

always immune from disease of any kind. Al- together, a highly-favoured plant.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0302.txt | included=true | included =====
Shyness Dying Out.

Shyness, in the olden times, often amounted to a malady, and youth suffered untold agonies from this physical form of self-consciousness. But in the present day the shy per Son is as scarce as hen's teeth, declares the "Boston Herald." Do you ever come . across a shy American child? Is there a young man who is ready to sink into the ground when he meet., a pretty girl, and does he wish he might die if she looks at him out of the tail of her • eye? Mercy, no. Re is as bold as brass. He couldn't blush to save himself, while as to the darling "buds," they bloom with absolute composure and are primed and equipped with vanity and swagger. And you must not blame them. It is in the air; a part of the new education which is so pragmatical and teeming with isms. Shyness has vanished from modern §ociOy.

FOOTBALL.

WAR-TIME FOOTBALL.

%%hint's Altitude Towards Suspension.

Mr, J. H. Smith, presiding at the annual in-eating of the Widnes Northern Union Foot: bug Cluir, said that last season the argument was advanced that they in the Northern Union could not survive- if they did not attempt to carry on. That orpiment did not apply now, and personally he did not believe that any hardshitOwould be suffered if football were sus- pended for the duration of the war. In fact clubs MI f4g; centres like Leeds and Manchester would nett- suffer at all.

The wholie question would be seriously con- sidered by the N.U. at their meeting on the Bth inst., anti! although he was speaking unoffi- cially he ratlittt4 thought that would be the atti- tude adopted. The Warrington President had been criticised for the stand he and the club had taken in deciding that as faeas they were concerned there should be no football next season.

They in Widnes had to consider the inter- ests of their own club and they were not pre- pared to spend the bank balance for which they had worked so hard, without very serious con- sideration. It might be that some scheme could be formulated by which amateur football could be played, but that would be a very difficult matter, because they could not prevent clubs paying surreptitiously.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0305.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTHERN UNION PLAYERS KILLED IN ACTION. -

News has been received of the deaths at the Front of two Northern Union players Phil Thomas, who was killed in action on Monday last, and Alfred Hopper, who was struck in the back by shrapnel. Thomas.was the well-known Welsh threequarter and Yorkshire County back, who for several seasons played with Hull Kingston Rovers. When Thomas left Wales he went first to Oldham and afterwards to Leeds. Hopper at one time played for the Hull Club..

===== 0002601_19150605_art0306.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSOCIATION.

Falling Off In " Final " Profits.

At a meeting of the Football Association on Monday Mr. J. C. Clegg presided, and the amount to be distributed among the clubs com- peting in the final and semi-final ties was given as follows:

Sheffield United (winners) Chelsea (runners-up) Bolton Wanderers (semi-finalists) .R176 is. 9d.

Everton (semi-finalists) To show how great a falling-off there is in the amount distributed as in other years, it may be mentioned that in 1912-13 the last four clubs took 0210,663 ss. 6d., Sunderland, who were run- ners-up, taking .t 4,580 Bs. 2d. Representatives of the four clubs met in con- ference and afterwards had a meeting with the F.A. Finance Committee on the subject of the sharing out. After some discussion they asked for the matter to be adjourned for a time, in the meantime further particulars to be supplied to them.

The secretary of one of the final clubs says that after they had paid compensation for dis- placed fixtures and bonuses to players for get- ting so far in the competition and expenses in connection with /the final tie they would be actually out of pocket to the extent of £lOO unless the F.A. saw their way to make a fur- ther grant.
