WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE.

On Monday night the annual meeting of the above League was held at the Lion Hotel, Mr. J. B. Starkey presiding. The gold medals won by the Fairfield United team as champions of the League for the past season were presented. Their record was:— Played, 20; won, 17; lost, 2; drawn, 1; goals scored f0r„,62; against, 14; points gained, 33. The club had two points deducted for playing an ,ineligibje. man. Mr. Starkey presented medals to the following players :—Potter, Gleave, Lewis, Long, S. Woodhouse and Cole. A. Woodhouse, W. Hunter, S. Bebbington and A. Short were not present. They have en- listed in the Army, and their medals were accepted by Mr. Clayton, the hon. secretary of the club.

Mr. Brandreth proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Starkey for presenting the medals. Mr. Clayton seconded the vote on behalf of the Fairfield club and players. Mr. Brandreth, the honorary secretary of the League, presented the balance sheet for the past season, which showed a balance in favour of the League of £5 9s. The Secre- tary, in his remarks on the season's working, made special mention of the loyalty of the clubs when they agreed to play in the " Starkey " CO Competition for the cup alone in order to try and put a balance to the League funds. This effort was largely respon- sible for the money now in hand.

On the proposition of Mr. Hatton, seconded by Mr. Phenix, the Secretary's report was unanimously adopted. Sir Gilbert Greenall was thanked for his ser- vices as President and the Secretary was in- structed to ask him to accept office as Patron to the League. Mr. J. B. Starkey was elected president.

Other elections: Treasurer, Mr-. R. Garner; chairman, Mr. H. Date; vice-chairman, Mr. Range; honorary secretary, Mr. R. Brandrtth, Orford, Warrington; bAulitors, Mr. W. Smith and Mr. Davies; sub-committee, Messrs. Wright. Smith and Essenhigh. Mr. P. Booth, of Daresbury, the late chair- man of the League, was elected a life member of the League for his past services. ' The following clubs were represented : Earlestown Mission, Elmwood Congregational- ists, Fairfield United, Grappenhall, Orford White star, Longford United, Paddington, and Hollins Green.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0308.txt | included=true | included =====
Recipe For Oratory.

Representative Hobson, at the end of a brilliant speech on naval armament, was con- gratulated on his oratory. "What is your recipe for good oratory?" a reporter asked. "I am afraid oratory comes natural," was the reply. "There is, though, one recipe for it, old Job Walmsby's, but it is hardly satis- factory. " Tha wants to be a public speyker, dos' tha, lad?' Job in his Yorkshire dialect, would say. 'An' tha thenks awm the chep to put tha up to .a wrinkle aboot it? Tha's right, I awm. Now, hark tha! When tha rises to meek thy speych, hit taable, and oppen thy mawth. If nowt comes, ta' a sup o' water, an' hit taable again. Then oppen thy mawth wider than afoor. Then, if nowt comes, tak' thysen off, and leave public spekin' to such as me.'

===== 0002601_19150605_art0309.txt | included=true | included =====
A Caution to Editors.

An editor in Reading (U.S.) advertised the other day that he "would take a good dog in payment of one year's subscription for his paper." The next day forty-three dogs were sent to the office. The day afterwards, when the news had spread into the,4 country, four hundred farmers sent two dogs apiece by ex- press, with eight baskets full of puppies, all marked " C.0.D." (Cash on Delivery). In the meantime the offer found, its way into the neighbouring States, And before the end of the week there -were eight thousand dogs, tied up with ropes, in the editor's - front and hack yards. The assortment included all kinds, from bloodhounds down to poodles. A few hundred broke loose and swarmed on the stair- ways and in the entries, and stood outside the sanctum and howled, and had fights, and sniffed under the crack of the door as if they were hungry for "some editor." And thr editor climbed out of the window, up the waterspout, and out on the comb of the roof, and wept. There was no issue of the paper for six days, and the only way in which the friends of the eminent journalist could feed him was by sending luncheon up to him in balloons.

Great Sankey

Local Delegate at Sons of Temperance

Conference.

Friendly Society Conference week during the Whitsuntide holidays saw the National Divi- sion of the Order of the Sons of Temperance conducting its annual business meetings at Leeds. The Warrington sub-division was repre- sented by Bro. W. Burrows, Grand Worthy Patriarch, of the Earlestown Grand Division, to which the branch at Warrington is attached. Bro. W. J. Whiteman, of London, was elected the Most Worthy Patriarch for the year. The report presented by the executive showed that the Order was in a flourishing condition. Splen- did service had been rendered in the totara-bsti- nen4 side of the work. During the past few years the Order had grown considerably, and at the close of 1914 there were 1,241 divisions, 1,145 sections, 118,262 adult ordinary members, 93,324 cadet members, and the funds totalled £603,017. The National Insurance side of the Order was of a satisfactory nature, the number of members for Great Britain being 126,367, this total showing an increase of 5,943 over the figures for 1913. In connection with the war, the report stated that 15,000 men belonging to the Order had responded to the call to arms, and along with Friendly Societies 'in general, the Sons of Temperance were maintaining all their " soldier " members in full finanCial bene- fit. Another item of interest provided by the report was in connection with the provision of recreation huts for soldiers in home camps. After a consultation with the Y.M.C.A. authori- ties, the executive made an appeal to the Grand Divisions, with the result that funds were secured which enabled the Order to erect a hut in the grounds of the Alexandra Palace, Lon- don, where it was formally opened on the 17th April.

WAR ITEMS.

MAKING GUNS INSTEAD OF GARDENS.

The Town Hall, Parks, and Baths commit- tee reported to the Town Council on Tuesday evening that they had referred the question of releasing some of the gardeners for the manufacture of munitions of war to the head gardener, with authority to release the men who decided to go if in his opinion they could be spared, and to make such temporary ar- rangements for carrying on the work as might seem desirable.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0313.txt | included=true | included =====
MIDSHIPMAN ALLEN SAVED.

Mr. Edwin Allen. of The Limes, Grappen- hall, received an official intimation about mid- night last Friday that his son, Midshipman Reginald Allen, who was on the Triumph, sunk in the Dardanelles earlier in the week, had been saved.

The town will unite in congratulating Past Grand Master Allen and Mrs. Allen upon the fact that their period of anxiety was so hap- pily and so promptly ended.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0314.txt | included=true | included =====
SCHOOL CARETAKER WHO 'LISTED.

At Tuesday night's meeting of the Warring- ton Town Council, Councillor Broadhurst said he noticed that the Education Committee litke granted permission to one of the school care- takers to join Kitchener's Army. Was any allowance to be made to the wife and depend- ents?--Councillor Roberts (the chairman of the commietee in question) replied that as the care- taker was a member of the Corporation staff he would undoubtedly come under the same cate- gory as others who had enlisted.

THE LATE LIEUT. ALTHORP RIDGWAY.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening, Alderman Bennett, in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Museum Conimittee, said a short time ago a resolution of sympathy was passed with Mr. 3ladeley, the curator, on the death of his wife. Now it was their duty to sympathise with the Chairman of the committee (Mr. T. J. Ridgway) on the death of his son. It must be a consolation to the parents to know that he died while fighting for his country. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19150605_art0315.txt | included=true | included =====
SECOND-LIEUT. HAMPSON.

Private J. H. J. Hampson (son of Mr. 3. H. J. Hampson, secretary to the Warrington In- firmary) who, soon after the outbreak of the war, left his desk in the office of Messrs. By- lands Bross to join the Lancashire Hussars, has been gazetted as a temporary second-lieu- tenant in the 10th Service Battalion South Lancashire Regiment.

Lieut. Hampson's great-grandfather fought at Waterloo and served 52 years in the Army; his grandfather was a soldier; and his father also served with the forces.

Four of his great uncles fought in the Cri- mean war, the Indian Mutiny and the China

war. Lieut. Hampson as a scholar at People's College won a grammar school scholarship.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0316.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAVES FOR SOLDIERS.

The Cemetery Committee reported to the Town Council on Tuesday evening that the sub-committee appointed to meet the Winwick Asylum authorities with regard to the inter- ment of soldiers reported that they had allo- cated a piece of land in the unconsecrated ground immediately adjoining the entrance gates of the cemetery, and they proposed that the piece of land should be sub-divided into three plots for the Church of England, Roman Catholic, and Nonconformist interments. The Town Clerk had interviewed the representa- tives of the different denominations and they were in agreement with this course; the Non- conformist bodies being of opinion that in the event of the different plots being separated by paths, the Nonconformist soldiers' graves shmild also be separated by a path from the Nonconformist civilian graves. It was proposed to charge the authorities 10s 6d. in respect of each interment this to include all fees. It was resolved that the action of the sub-committee be proved and that application be made to the Home Secretary for-his sanction to the scheme and that the Town Clerk take the necessary steps to have the Church of England portion consecrated.

===== 0002601_19150605_art0317.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR RADDON COURT RED CROSS

HOSPITAL.

Pleasing Exhibiiion Of Fancy Dancing

In the Church Room, Stockton Heath, on Monday evening, the pupils of Miss Gertrude Brown gave a very pretty exhibition of fancy dancing, the proceeds of the entertainment being • in aid of the Raddon Court Red Cross Hospital. There was a good attendance. The programme was opened by a scarf dance by the Misses Houghton, Southerden, Domville and Tyrer, who also gave a coon dance and gavotte, all of which were executed with the requisite verve and grace. The quartet also gave a sailor's horn- pipe, accompanied by Miss Cooper and Miss Joan Cooper. Miss Hilda Domville executed a, pretty fan dance, while a very successful taran- tella was given by the Misses Dorothy Tyrer and Mildred Southerden, dressed as gipsies. Miss Southerden's dance entitled "Reverie" was splendidly performed, while the Misses Tyrer and Minnie Houghton were seen to great advan- tage in a pierette and pierrot flirtation dance. Miss Tyrer also executed the Highland fling with much sprightliness. Distinct success marked the performance of an Irish jig by the Misses Hilda Domville and Dorothy Tyrer. Other items included two songs by Miss Elsie Hewitt—", The Smile Qf Spring" and "May Morning "—and Mr. W. Booth sang "I hear you calling me" and " Thorn " very pleasingly. "Ye banks and braes" and "0 lovely night" were two cornet duets given by Mrs. Willett and Mr. Rutter. Mr. Eric Jeffery recited with much effect, while Miss Corney was seen to advantage in a monologue. Miss Parkinson and Mr. W. H. Payton were the accompanists. During an interval in the programme Mr. W. Brown thanked all who had contributed to the success of the entertainment.

Public Notices. ' COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. JUNIOR CLERKSHIPS IN CORPORATION DEPARTMENTS. An EXAMINATION for the purpose of form- ing a "Waiting List" for appointments to Junior Clerkships in Corporation Offices during the twelve months commencing Ist August, 1915, will he/held in the month of July next. Vacan- cies, not filled by promotion, occurring after the date mentioned, will be filled from the "Waiting List" of candidates who have obtained not less than 60 per cent. of marks. Before appoint- ment Candidates will be required to submit themselves to an examination by the Medical Officer of Health.. Candidates obtaining not less than 60 per cent. of marks will be furnished with a certificate to that effect. Applicants must be the sons of ratepayers of the Borough, and must not be less than 14 or more than 15i years of age on the Ist AuFust, 1915. ' Forms of application ma' be obtained frOm the Town Clerk's Office, and no Candidate will be admitted for examination unless he has returned the form, duly completed, not later than noon on Thursday, the 17th day of June, 1915. . Dated this 4th day of June, 1915. LYON WHITTLE, - • Town Clerk. 1 Town Hall, Warrington.

PRIVATE E. JACKSON. Wounded.

Schools, since leaving which he has assisted his father in the conduct of a newsagent's business. . Six years a "Terrier," he specially interested himself in signalling, for which he secured a first-class badge. He was deeply interested in the Boy Scout movement and had acted as Assistant Scoutmaster of the Ist Warrington Troop. He was a regular wor- shipper at St. Ann's Church, prior to going to the Front. • • SERGEANT W. GRIFFITHS. "Non-Corn." Who Rejoined As A Private.

SERGEANT W. GRIFFITHS, Wounded. On Thursday Mrs. Griffiths, of 145, Orford- lane, learned that her husband, Sergeant Walter Griffiths, of the Warrington Terri- torials, had been wounded in action the pre- vious Saturday. He was hit in the leg. Sergeant Griffiths, who is thirty years of age, served in the old Volugteers and at the out- break of war joined the Territorials as a private, and was speedily rehabilitated in his old rank. In times of peace he worked for Messrs. Crosfield, and was, for many years, a member of the famous band in connection with the firm. He was educated at the National School, in Church-street, and went to the Front with the first batch of Terri- torials. PRIVATE A. ROBERTS. Shot Whilst Asleep In A hint. Roberts, of the Warrington Territorials, who wrote to his mother, Mrs. W. Roberts, at 56, Chorley-street, Warrington, after he had been wounded. Private Roberts, who joined the Territorials for the first time at the outbreak of war, was, at the time, employed at Rylands's. PRIVATE A. J. WOOD. Wounded.

friend, Private W. Mullin, a stretcher-bearer attached to B Company. "Just a line to inform you that your son George has been wounded in the foot. He was shot by my side at 11.30 p.m. on May 12th. I am glad to say that it is not a very bad wound. You may see him in a week or two. I dressed him and brought him to Dr. Law to be attended to in the hospital, and\he is doing fine. I am sorry for him; for he was a quiet lad and a good mate." •••••••••••••••••••••••••••••••••••• PRIVATE JAMES LIPTROTT. "Thought His Head Was knocked Off." News reached Mrs. Liptrott, last week, at 65, Lythgoe's-lane, that her .husband, Private James Liptrott, of " D " Cdrapany, of the War- rington Territorials, had been wounded. Hit in the Neck by Shrapnel. Intimation was first received through the medium of a letter written by Private N. Millington, also of the Territorials. The letter was as follows :- "Jim got wounded last night in the neck by shrapnel, and hits gone to the base. He was all right when I left him, and was up, smok- ing. It is not much, so you have no need to worry, for he will be all right. I hope he is back soon, as I feel lost without him. He may get to England, but I do not think so, as it is not serious. He told rue to write to you. I was next to him, and I nearly 'got it,' too. I would not have cared if I had gone with him." "It Was A Wizard' That Did It." Later, Mrs. Liptrott received the following letter from her husband, who wrote from No. 2 General Hospital, Le Havre, France : "I will soon be all right," he said. "We are in a fine place on the coast. I thought my head was knocked off when 1 was hit.- It was

TERRITORIAL ROLL OF HONOUR.

LANCE-CORPORAL H. LEAH. "Like Being In Heaven To Lie On A Soft Bed." Lance-corporal Leah, son of Mr. and Mrs. Leah of 10, Miler-street, was wounded on Saturday May 22nd. He himself broke the news to his parents, through the medium of a field post-card. A Bullet Through The Arm-Muscle. Later, under date May 23rd, he sent the fol- lowing letter:— "I am going on fine, and am in the pink. I dare say you got m.y field card all' right tel- ling you that I had been trying to stop a bul- let, but it went right through the muscle of . my right arm, missing the bone, and through another chap's right arm, so that ,you see it

f, • I°. , • , >••••`, 4 , , • • `,14cri51,,,, * 1 t,14.81" . ”#>‘`' 'Y • " ""i" 111 Wounded. • LANCE-CORPORAL H. LEAH.

got two of us. We were coming back from the trenches after taking water for some of boys, and I was just thinking to myself that we had got back without any of us being hit, when this One came right behind us, • so we got both sent down to the hospital at Rouen. We are looked after fine. It is like being in heaven lying on a soft bed and a clean rig- out on. The wound is nothing, so you have no need to worry yourselves over it. The sisters here are very good to us. To-day we had a concert in the garden, and we looked smart in our grey rig-out and red mufflers." An Old Footballer. Lance-corporal Leah, is a single man, 22 years of age, and makes his home with his parents at 10 Milner-street. , He has been a member of the Territorials for four years, and is at present attached to A. Company of the Territorials. He was educated at the Church of the Sacred Heart school, and up to the out- break -of war he was employed at the Dallam- lane Brewery. At one time he played for the Warrington r xcelsior Football club, in the Warrington 'and District League. Lance- corporal Leah's father, Mr. William Leah, works at Messrs. Boots the Cash Chemists.

PRIVATE IlAilitY RHODES: -- "Hit In The Left Ear By .1 German Bullet." • Private Harry Rhodes, of the *arrington Ter- ritorials, is among those who have been wounded in action. A letter, which was dated Thurs- day, Max 27th, and was received by his mother, who lives at 101, Slater-street, Latchford, con- veyed the news. It ran as follows : A Surprise when on Sentry Duty. "I am getting on all right and in good health. No doubt you will be surprised to hear that I got hit in the left ear by a Ger- man bullet. I was on sentry duty about three o'clock on Wednesday Morning. I had just had a look over the parapet and was

. , " , , , . , „ • • _ S. t , , , 5; , „ • • • , . , , • .

stepping down, when I got hit. The bullet went through the top of my ear, taking a small piece off it. It was a lucky thing for me that it was not another half an inch to the right, for if it had been so, I would not have been writing this letter. As it is, hoW- ever, it is not very much, for I was able. walk to the dressing station with full pack on. . . . When I was at the dressing station, Major Crosfield was very kirld to me. Ido not know what we should do without him! . . We only went into the trenches on Tuesday night, and we had some lovely weather for the last fortnight. I shall be able to join my company in a day or two." Time-expired "Terrier," but Re-joined when Call Came." Private Rhodes is a married man and resides in peace times at Oldham-street, Latchford. He is 24 years of age, and is a member of C Com- pany. His time of service in the Territorials had expired, but upon the outbreak of war, he left Messrs. Richmond's, where he was em- ployed as a moulder, and re-joined. As a boy, he attended St. James' day school, and also the Sunday • school held in connection With St. James' Church. He is Very fond of football and boxing.

PRIVATE WILLIAM BOWNESS. Wounded. ON HONOUR'S SCROLL. DIED. OF WOUNDS. ABLE SEAMAN THOMAS WOOD. Hit Whilst In Action Near The Dardanelles. News has been received by .Mrs. Wood, who lives at 55, Wildertspool-causeway, that her son, Able Seaman Thomas Wood, died in hos- pital at Alexandria on Thursday of last ,week, from wounds received during the fighting in the vicinity of the Dardanelles. The first official intimation received stated that he was wounded. This intimation, which came from the War Office, read as follows: " I regret to inform you that your son, A.S. Thomas Wood, K.W./350, It.N.V.R., was wounded in action near the Dardanelles on the 17th May/1915. Any further informa- tion that is received shall be posted to you at once."

ABLE SEAMAN T. WOOD. Died of Wounds. The letter was signed by Lieut. A. Sandall Wells, R.N.V.R. News 01 His Death. Later Mrs. Wood received the following message "I deeply regret to inform you that a tele- gram has been received here to-day announc- ing the death of your son, Able Seaman Wood, 350, R.N.V.R.—who was serving in the Drake Battalion, Ist R.N. Brigade, R.N. Divi- sion—from wounds received in action near the Dardanelles, of which news was sent you on the 18th inst.' This communication was also signed by Lieutenant Wells. From Army to Navy. Able Seaman Wood was a single man, twenty years of age. He formerly attended Christ Church Day School, Latchford, and was em- ployed at the Latchford Tube Works. It was in September that he enlisted in the York- shire Light Infantry, hut after a few months he was transferred to the Royal Naval Volun- teer Reserve, and it. was while serving with this Force that he met his death. ,He wor- shipped at St. John's Presbyterian Church. RIFLEMAN M. GLYNN. Billed in Action.

Official news has now. been received of the death of Rifleman Michael Glynn, 14, Philip- street, of the' Ist. Rifle Brigade, a labourer at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, who, as reported in last week's "Examiner" was killed in action May 14th. Rifleman Glynn and some comrades were :chasing a number of Germans back to their own trenches. Very few of the enemy got back. One or two :must have remained in the trenches for as soon as the enemy regained cover Glynn was shot from a loophole and died instantly. Ile was 20 yetirs of age and attended St. Alban's school and church.
